    PHASE 2 -- Business Analysis Techniques & Requirement Analysis

    Lesson 10: Requirement Engineering Fundamentals

    Requirements are the bridge between a business problem and a successful solution.

    A BA\'s main responsibility is not just writing documents.

    A BA must ensure:

    Business Need → Requirement → Solution → Business Value

    Learning Objectives

    By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

    • What requirement engineering is

    • Why requirement engineering matters

    • Requirement lifecycle

    • Types of requirements

    • Requirement hierarchy

    • Business vs Stakeholder vs Solution requirements

    • Functional vs Non-Functional requirements

    • Transition requirements

    • Healthcare AI examples

    1. What is Requirement Engineering?

    Definition:

    Requirement Engineering is the systematic process of discovering, analyzing, documenting, validating, managing, and controlling requirements throughout a project lifecycle.

    Simple:

    It is the process of understanding:

    • What business needs

    • Why it needs it

    • What solution should provide

    • How success will be measured

    Requirement Engineering Lifecycle

    Business Need

    ↓

    Elicitation

    ↓

    Analysis

    ↓

    Documentation

    ↓

    Validation

    ↓

    Management

    ↓

    Solution Delivery

    2. Why Requirement Engineering Is Important

    Many project failures happen because requirements are unclear.

    Example:

    Business says:

    \"We need AI automation.\"

    Developer builds:

    AI chatbot.

    But business actually wanted:

    AI-based adverse event extraction.

    Problem:

    Poor requirement understanding.

    Good requirement engineering prevents:

    ❌ Wrong solution\ ❌ Rework\ ❌ Cost overruns\ ❌ Delayed delivery\ ❌ Stakeholder dissatisfaction

    3. Requirement vs Feature vs Solution

    Very important distinction.

    Business Problem

    Why change is needed.

    Example:

    Safety reviewers spend too much time processing cases.

    Requirement

    What capability is needed.

    Example:

    System should extract adverse event information automatically.

    Feature

    Product capability.

    Example:

    AI Document Extraction Module.

    Solution

    Implementation.

    Example:

    LLM + NLP pipeline integrated with safety database.

    Flow:

    Problem

    ↓

    Requirement

    ↓

    Feature

    ↓

    Solution

    4. Requirement Hierarchy

    Professional BAs organize requirements in levels.

    Level 1: Business Requirements

    Question:

    WHY?

    Defines business objectives.

    Example:

    \"Reduce pharmacovigilance case processing time by 50%.\"

    Created by:

    • Business owners

    • Executives

    • Product managers

    Level 2: Stakeholder Requirements

    Question:

    WHAT do stakeholders need?

    Defines user expectations.

    Example:

    \"Safety reviewers need faster case summarization.\"

    Created from:

    • Interviews

    • Workshops

    • Research

    Level 3: Solution Requirements

    Question:

    WHAT should the solution do?

    Divided into:

    • Functional requirements

    • Non-functional requirements

    Example:

    \"The system should generate a case summary using AI.\"

    Level 4: Transition Requirements

    Question:

    What is needed to move from current state to future state?

    Example:

    • User training

    • Data migration

    • System integration

    Requirement Hierarchy Diagram

    Business Requirement

    ↓

    Stakeholder Requirement

    ↓

    Solution Requirement

    ↓

    Functional + Non Functional

    ↓

    Transition Requirement

    5. Types of Requirements

    A BA must understand four major categories.

    1. Business Requirements

    Explain business goals.

    Example:

    \"Reduce manual safety review effort.\"

    Characteristics:

    • High level

    • Business focused

    • Outcome oriented

    2. Stakeholder Requirements

    Explain stakeholder needs.

    Example:

    \"Reviewer needs AI-generated summaries.\"

    Characteristics:

    • User focused

    • Describes expectations

    3. Solution Requirements

    Describe solution capabilities.

    Two types:

    Functional Requirements

    What the system should do.

    Non-Functional Requirements

    How the system should perform.

    4. Transition Requirements

    Temporary requirements needed during change.

    Examples:

    • Data migration

    • Training

    • New SOP creation

    6. Functional Requirements (FR)

    Definition:

    Functional requirements describe specific behaviors or capabilities that a system must provide.

    They answer:

    \"What should the system do?\"

    Examples:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Platform:

    FR1:

    \"The system shall extract patient information from uploaded documents.\"

    FR2:

    \"The system shall generate an adverse event summary.\"

    FR3:

    \"The system shall allow reviewers to approve or modify AI suggestions.\"

    Functional requirements usually start with:

    \"The system shall...\"

    7. Non-Functional Requirements (NFR)

    Definition:

    Non-functional requirements describe quality attributes and constraints of the system.

    They answer:

    \"How well should the system work?\"

    Common NFR categories:

    Performance

    Example:

    \"System response time should be less than 3 seconds.\"

    Security

    Example:

    \"User authentication must use multi-factor authentication.\"

    Reliability

    Example:

    \"System availability should be 99.9%.\"

    Scalability

    Example:

    \"System should support 10,000 users.\"

    Usability

    Example:

    \"Users should complete case review within 5 minutes.\"

    Compliance

    Example:

    \"System must maintain audit logs.\"

    Functional vs Non-Functional Example

    Requirement:

    \"AI summary should be generated.\"

    Functional:

    ✅ System generates summary.

    Non-functional:

    ✅ Summary generated within 5 seconds.

    8. Transition Requirements

    Transition requirements help move from current state to future state.

    Example:

    Implementing AI Pharmacovigilance Platform.

    Current:

    Manual review.

    Future:

    AI-assisted review.

    Transition needs:

    Requirement Purpose

    Data migration Move historical cases

    User training Teach AI workflow

    SOP update Define new process

    Validation Ensure compliance

    9. Requirement Quality Characteristics

    Good requirements should be:

    Clear

    Easy to understand.

    Complete

    No missing information.

    Consistent

    No conflicts.

    Testable

    Can verify whether achieved.

    Feasible

    Can realistically be implemented.

    Traceable

    Can track from business need to solution.

    10. Requirement Traceability

    Traceability connects:

    Business objective → Requirement → Feature → Test Case

    Example:

    Business Goal

    Reduce review time

    ↓

    Requirement

    AI generates summaries

    ↓

    Feature

    Summary Engine

    ↓

    Test Case

    Verify summary accuracy

    11. Healthcare AI Example

    Business Requirement

    Reduce adverse event processing time.

    ↓

    Stakeholder Requirement

    Safety reviewers need automated information extraction.

    ↓

    Functional Requirement

    System shall extract patient demographics and event details.

    ↓

    Non-Functional Requirement

    System shall maintain audit trail for all AI outputs.

    ↓

    Transition Requirement

    Users must complete AI workflow training.

    12. Interview Questions

    Q1: What is requirement engineering?

    Answer:

    \"Requirement engineering is the process of eliciting, analyzing, documenting, validating, and managing requirements to ensure the solution meets business needs.\"

    Q2: Difference between functional and non-functional requirements?

    Answer:

    \"Functional requirements define what the system should do, while non-functional requirements define how well the system should perform.\"

    Q3: What are transition requirements?

    Answer:

    \"Transition requirements describe temporary capabilities needed to move from the current state to the future state, such as training, migration, and process changes.\"

    Practical Assignment

    Create requirement hierarchy for:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

    Write:

    1. Business Requirement

    (Why?)

    2. Stakeholder Requirements

    (Who needs what?)

    3. Functional Requirements

    (What should system do?)

    Minimum 10 FRs.

    4. Non-Functional Requirements

    Minimum 10 NFRs.

    5. Transition Requirements

    (What is needed during implementation?)

    Lesson Summary

    You learned:

    ✅ Requirement Engineering concept\ ✅ Requirement lifecycle\ ✅ Requirement hierarchy\ ✅ Business requirements\ ✅ Stakeholder requirements\ ✅ Solution requirements\ ✅ Functional requirements\ ✅ Non-functional requirements\ ✅ Transition requirements\ ✅ Traceability basics

    PHASE 2 -- Business Analysis Techniques & Requirement Analysis

    Lesson 11: Requirement Elicitation Masterclass

    A requirement is not collected; it is discovered.

    Many beginners think:

    \"Stakeholder tells requirements → BA writes them.\"

    A professional BA knows:

    Stakeholders usually describe solutions, not actual needs.

    Example:

    Stakeholder:

    \"We need a chatbot.\"

    BA does not immediately write:

    \"Build chatbot.\"

    BA investigates:

    • Why do they need it?

    • What problem exists?

    • Who will use it?

    • What outcome is expected?

    Learning Objectives

    By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

    • What requirement elicitation is

    • Why elicitation is important

    • Requirement elicitation process

    • Different elicitation techniques

    • Interview techniques

    • Workshop facilitation

    • Observation technique

    • Document analysis

    • Prototyping

    • Healthcare AI examples

    1. What is Requirement Elicitation?

    Definition:

    Requirement elicitation is the process of discovering, collecting, and understanding requirements from stakeholders, users, documents, systems, and business processes.

    Simple:

    Finding answers to:

    • What does the business need?

    • What problems do users face?

    • What capabilities are required?

    2. Why Requirement Elicitation Matters

    Poor elicitation causes:

    ❌ Missing requirements\ ❌ Wrong assumptions\ ❌ Scope changes\ ❌ Rework\ ❌ Failed products

    Example:

    A pharma company says:

    \"We want AI automation.\"

    Without elicitation:

    Team builds:

    AI chatbot.

    After proper elicitation:

    Discovery:

    Actual need:

    AI should extract adverse event data from reports.

    3. Requirement Elicitation Process

    A BA follows:

    Prepare

    ↓

    Identify Stakeholders

    ↓

    Select Techniques

    ↓

    Conduct Elicitation

    ↓

    Document Findings

    ↓

    Validate Requirements

    Step 1: Prepare for Elicitation

    Before meeting stakeholders:

    BA understands:

    • Business objective

    • Existing documents

    • Current process

    • Stakeholder roles

    Example:

    Before interviewing safety reviewers:

    Review:

    • SOP documents

    • Case workflow

    • Existing system screens

    Step 2: Identify Stakeholders

    Ask:

    Who knows the process?

    Who uses the system?

    Who approves decisions?

    Who is impacted?

    Example:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Platform:

    Stakeholder Why involved

    Safety Reviewer End user

    Medical Reviewer Clinical validation

    Regulatory Team Compliance

    IT Team Integration

    Product Owner Business value

    Step 3: Select Elicitation Technique

    Different situations need different methods.

    Technique 1: Stakeholder Interviews

    Most common BA technique.

    Definition:

    A structured conversation to gather information.

    Types:

    Structured Interview

    Fixed questions.

    Example:

    Survey style.

    Semi-Structured Interview

    Prepared questions + discussion.

    Most commonly used.

    Unstructured Interview

    Open conversation.

    Used during discovery.

    Interview Questions Example

    For Safety Reviewer:

    Current Process

    \"How do you currently review cases?\"

    Pain Points

    \"What takes the most time?\"

    Improvement

    \"What would make your work easier?\"

    Validation

    \"What risks should AI avoid?\"

    Interview Best Practices

    Before:

    ✅ Prepare questions\ ✅ Understand stakeholder role

    During:

    ✅ Listen more than talk\ ✅ Ask follow-up questions\ ✅ Capture examples

    After:

    ✅ Confirm understanding\ ✅ Document findings

    Technique 2: Workshops

    Definition:

    A collaborative session where multiple stakeholders discuss and define requirements.

    Used when:

    • Many stakeholders exist

    • Conflicts need resolution

    • Process understanding is required

    Example:

    AI Workflow Workshop:

    Participants:

    • Safety reviewers

    • Medical experts

    • IT

    • Compliance

    Discuss:

    Current workflow → Future workflow

    Workshop Agenda Example

    1. Introduction

    Explain objective.

    2. Current State Discussion

    Understand problems.

    3. Future State Discussion

    Define desired capabilities.

    4. Requirement Capture

    Document needs.

    5. Agreement

    Confirm decisions.

    Technique 3: Brainstorming

    Purpose:

    Generate ideas.

    Rules:

    • No immediate criticism

    • Encourage ideas

    • Group similar ideas

    Example:

    Question:

    \"How can AI improve safety operations?\"

    Ideas:

    • Auto-summary

    • Risk prediction

    • Duplicate detection

    • Document extraction

    Technique 4: Observation

    Definition:

    BA watches users perform real work.

    Why useful?

    Users may not explain everything.

    Example:

    A reviewer says:

    \"Case processing is simple.\"

    Observation shows:

    They switch between 5 systems.

    Discovery:

    Integration requirement.

    Technique 5: Document Analysis

    BA reviews existing information.

    Sources:

    • SOPs

    • Policies

    • Reports

    • Existing requirements

    • User manuals

    • Regulatory documents

    Example:

    Review:

    Pharmacovigilance SOP.

    Discover:

    Mandatory approval steps.

    Technique 6: Surveys / Questionnaires

    Useful when:

    Many users are involved.

    Example:

    100 reviewers across locations.

    Questions:

    • Current challenges

    • Feature expectations

    • Satisfaction level

    Technique 7: Focus Groups

    Small group discussion.

    Useful for:

    Understanding user opinions.

    Example:

    Patients discussing medication tracking app.

    Technique 8: Prototyping

    One of the most powerful techniques.

    Definition:

    Creating a visual model of the solution to collect feedback.

    Examples:

    • Wireframes

    • Mockups

    • Clickable prototypes

    Example:

    Instead of explaining AI dashboard:

    Show prototype.

    Ask:

    \"Would this workflow help you?\"

    4. Elicitation Technique Selection Guide

    Situation Best Technique

    Understand user problems Interview

    Multiple stakeholders Workshop

    Large user group Survey

    Understand actual work Observation

    Existing system Document Analysis

    Validate solution idea Prototype

    Generate ideas Brainstorming

    5. Requirement Elicitation Challenges

    Challenge 1:

    Stakeholder does not know requirements.

    Solution:

    Use:

    • Interviews

    • Prototypes

    • Examples

    Challenge 2:

    Conflicting requirements.

    Solution:

    • Understand goals

    • Analyze impact

    • Prioritize

    Challenge 3:

    Stakeholders are unavailable.

    Solution:

    • Review documents

    • Use existing data

    • Conduct asynchronous surveys

    6. AI Product Elicitation Example

    Project:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

    Interview Discovery:

    Reviewer says:

    \"I spend too much time reading narratives.\"

    Observation:

    BA observes:

    Reviewer copies data between systems.

    Workshop:

    Team identifies:

    Need:

    AI extraction + integration.

    Prototype:

    BA creates:

    AI summary screen.

    Final Requirements:

    • Extract case information

    • Generate summary

    • Allow human editing

    • Maintain audit trail

    7. Elicitation Documentation

    BA captures:

    Requirement Notes

    What was discussed.

    Business Rules

    Constraints.

    Example:

    \"Only qualified reviewers can approve cases.\"

    Assumptions

    Example:

    \"Historical cases are available.\"

    Issues

    Example:

    \"Data quality needs improvement.\"

    Decisions

    Example:

    \"Human approval remains mandatory.\"

    8. Interview Questions

    Q1: What is requirement elicitation?

    Answer:

    \"Requirement elicitation is the process of discovering requirements from stakeholders, users, documents, systems, and business processes using different techniques.\"

    Q2: Which elicitation techniques have you used?

    Answer:

    \"I use interviews, workshops, document analysis, observation, brainstorming, surveys, and prototyping depending on project needs.\"

    Q3: How do you handle unclear requirements?

    Answer:

    \"I ask probing questions, analyze the underlying business problem, use prototypes where needed, and validate understanding with stakeholders.\"

    Practical Assignment

    For your:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

    Create an Elicitation Plan.

    Include:

    1. Stakeholders

    Example:

    • PV Reviewer

    • Medical Reviewer

    • Regulatory Team

    • IT Team

    2. Technique Selection

    For each stakeholder:

    • Interview?

    • Workshop?

    • Observation?

    • Prototype?

    3. Questions

    Create 5 questions for each stakeholder.

    ✅ Requirement elicitation meaning\ ✅ Elicitation process\ ✅ Stakeholder identification\ ✅ Interviews\ ✅ Workshops\ ✅ Brainstorming\ ✅ Observation\ ✅ Document analysis\ ✅ Surveys\ ✅ Prototyping\ ✅ Healthcare AI example

    PHASE 2 -- Business Analysis Techniques & Requirement Analysis

    Lesson 12: Requirement Analysis & Documentation Masterclass

    Requirement elicitation discovers what stakeholders need. Requirement analysis converts those needs into clear, structured, and usable requirements.

    A BA\'s job is not to copy stakeholder statements.

    Stakeholder says:

    \"We need a smart AI system.\"

    BA transforms it into:

    • Business objective

    • Functional capability

    • User requirement

    • Acceptance criteria

    • Business rules

    • Constraints

    Learning Objectives

    By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

    • Requirement analysis process

    • Requirement documentation hierarchy

    • BRD

    • FRD

    • SRS

    • User Stories

    • Use Cases

    • Acceptance Criteria

    • Business Rules

    • Decision Tables

    • Requirement Traceability

    1. What is Requirement Analysis?

    Definition:

    Requirement Analysis is the process of examining, refining, organizing, prioritizing, and validating requirements to ensure they are complete, clear, feasible, and aligned with business goals.

    Simple:

    Elicitation asks:

    \"What do stakeholders need?\"

    Analysis asks:

    \"What exactly does that mean?\"

    Example

    Stakeholder Statement:

    \"The system should be intelligent.\"

    BA Analysis:

    What does intelligent mean?

    Possible requirements:

    • Extract information automatically

    • Identify missing fields

    • Generate summaries

    • Recommend actions

    2. Requirement Analysis Process

    id=\"7q5f8v\"

    Collected Requirements

    ↓

    Analyze & Clarify

    ↓

    Remove Ambiguity

    ↓

    Prioritize

    ↓

    Model Requirements

    ↓

    Validate

    ↓

    Document

    3. Requirement Analysis Activities

    A BA performs:

    1. Categorization

    Classify requirements:

    • Business

    • Functional

    • Non-functional

    • Transition

    2. Decomposition

    Break large requirements into smaller ones.

    Example:

    Large:

    \"Build AI Pharmacovigilance Platform.\"

    Break into:

    • Document upload

    • Data extraction

    • Case classification

    • Summary generation

    • Approval workflow

    3. Prioritization

    Determine importance.

    Methods:

    • MoSCoW

    • Business Value

    • Risk

    • Cost

    4. Validation

    Confirm:

    \"Did we understand correctly?\"

    5. Documentation

    Create professional artifacts.

    4. Requirement Documentation Hierarchy

    A professional project may contain:

    id=\"p7h9z3\"

    Business Need

    ↓

    Business Requirement Document (BRD)

    ↓

    Functional Requirement Document (FRD)

    ↓

    Software Requirement Specification (SRS)

    ↓

    User Stories

    ↓

    Acceptance Criteria

    ↓

    Test Cases

    5. BRD -- Business Requirement Document

    What is BRD?

    BRD describes:

    Why the project exists.

    Focus:

    Business goals and objectives.

    Usually created by:

    • Business Analyst

    • Product Manager

    • Business Owner

    BRD Structure

    1. Introduction

    Project background.

    2. Business Objective

    Example:

    Reduce PV case processing time by 50%.

    3. Current Problem

    Example:

    Manual review causes delays.

    4. Scope

    In scope:

    AI extraction.

    Out of scope:

    Fully automated approval.

    5. Stakeholders

    List involved parties.

    6. Business Requirements

    Example:

    \"The organization requires an AI-assisted safety review capability.\"

    7. Success Criteria

    Example:

    • Reduce processing time

    • Improve accuracy

    BRD Example

    Project:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

    Business Requirement:

    The organization needs an AI-enabled platform to reduce manual adverse event processing effort while maintaining regulatory compliance.

    6. FRD -- Functional Requirement Document

    What is FRD?

    FRD describes:

    What the system should do.

    Focus:

    System behavior.

    FRD Example

    Functional Requirement:

    FR-001

    Name:

    AI Document Extraction

    Description:

    The system shall extract patient and adverse event information from uploaded documents.

    Additional details:

    Input:

    PDF reports

    Process:

    AI extraction

    Output:

    Structured case data

    FRD Structure

    1. Functional Requirements

    System features.

    2. User Roles

    Example:

    • Reviewer

    • Manager

    • Administrator

    3. Process Flow

    Workflow diagrams.

    4. Business Rules

    Constraints.

    5. Error Handling

    Expected system behavior.

    7. SRS -- Software Requirement Specification

    SRS is a more technical requirement document.

    Usually used by:

    • Software teams

    • Architects

    • QA teams

    Contains:

    Functional Requirements

    What system does.

    Non-Functional Requirements

    How system performs.

    Interfaces

    System integrations.

    Security

    Access control.

    Performance

    Response time.

    BRD vs FRD vs SRS

    Document Focus Question

    BRD Business Why?

    FRD Functionality What?

    SRS Software details How system behaves?

    8. User Stories

    Common in Agile.

    Format:

    As a \<user>

    I want \<capability>

    So that \<benefit>

    Example:

    As a pharmacovigilance reviewer, I want AI-generated case summaries so that I can reduce manual review time.

    User Story Components

    User

    Who needs it?

    Action

    What do they want?

    Benefit

    Why?

    9. Acceptance Criteria

    Acceptance criteria define:

    \"When is this requirement complete?\"

    Example User Story:

    As a reviewer, I want AI summaries.

    Acceptance Criteria:

    Given:

    A case document is uploaded.

    When:

    The reviewer requests summary generation.

    Then:

    The system generates a summary within 10 seconds.

    Acceptance Criteria Format

    Given

    Initial condition.

    When

    Action performed.

    Then

    Expected result.

    Called:

    Gherkin Format

    Example:

    Given the user uploads a PDF report

    When AI extraction starts

    Then patient information should be extracted

    And displayed for reviewer validation

    10. Business Rules

    Definition:

    Business rules define policies, constraints, or decisions that the system must follow.

    Examples:

    Rule 1:

    Only qualified safety reviewers can approve cases.

    Rule 2:

    AI-generated content must be reviewed before submission.

    Rule 3:

    Critical adverse events require escalation.

    11. Decision Tables

    Used when multiple conditions create different outcomes.

    Example:

    Case Escalation Rule

    Severity Serious? Action

    High Yes Immediate escalation

    High No Normal review

    Low Yes Review required

    Low No Routine processing

    12. Requirement Traceability Matrix (RTM)

    RTM connects:

    Requirement → Design → Development → Testing

    Example:

    Req ID Requirement Test Case

    FR-001 Extract data TC-001

    FR-002 Generate summary TC-002

    Benefits:

    • No requirement missed

    • Easy change management

    • Better testing

    13. Healthcare AI Example

    Business Requirement

    Reduce manual safety review effort.

    ↓

    Functional Requirement

    System shall extract adverse event information.

    ↓

    User Story

    As a reviewer, I want extracted information so that I reduce manual entry.

    ↓

    Acceptance Criteria

    Given uploaded report

    When extraction runs

    Then structured data is generated.

    ↓

    Test Case

    Verify extraction accuracy.

    14. Interview Questions

    Q1: Difference between BRD and FRD?

    Answer:

    \"BRD explains business objectives and needs, while FRD describes the functional behavior required from the solution.\"

    Q2: What makes a good requirement?

    Answer:

    \"A good requirement should be clear, complete, consistent, feasible, testable, and traceable.\"

    Q3: What are acceptance criteria?

    Answer:

    \"Acceptance criteria define the conditions that must be satisfied for a user story or requirement to be considered complete.\"

    Practical Assignment

    Create documentation for:

    Feature:

    AI Adverse Event Summary Generator

    Prepare:

    1. BRD Section

    • Business objective

    • Problem statement

    • Success criteria

    2. FRD

    Create:

    • 5 functional requirements

    3. User Stories

    Create:

    • 3 user stories

    4. Acceptance Criteria

    Create:

    • Given/When/Then criteria

    5. Business Rules

    Create:

    • 5 rules

    Lesson Summary

    You learned:

    ✅ Requirement analysis\ ✅ BRD\ ✅ FRD\ ✅ SRS\ ✅ User Stories\ ✅ Acceptance Criteria\ ✅ Business Rules\ ✅ Decision Tables\ ✅ RTM

    Mini Example of Documents

    AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot Platform

    Scenario:

    A pharmaceutical company wants to reduce manual adverse event case processing using AI.

    Document 1: BRD (Business Requirement Document)

    Purpose of BRD

    BRD answers:

    WHY are we building this solution?

    It focuses on business problems, objectives, scope, stakeholders, and expected outcomes.

    BRD Example

    Business Requirement Document

    Project Name

    AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

    1. Business Background

    The pharmaceutical company receives thousands of adverse event reports from multiple sources including healthcare professionals, patients, and regulatory submissions.

    Currently, safety reviewers manually extract information, classify events, and prepare narratives.

    This causes:

    • High processing time

    • Increased operational workload

    • Risk of delays

    2. Business Problem Statement

    Current pharmacovigilance case processing requires significant manual effort.

    Safety reviewers spend considerable time extracting information from documents, reducing the time available for safety assessment.

    3. Business Objective

    The objective is to implement an AI-assisted pharmacovigilance platform that:

    • Reduces manual data extraction effort

    • Improves case processing speed

    • Maintains regulatory compliance

    • Supports safety reviewers with AI assistance

    4. Project Scope

    In Scope

    ✅ Upload adverse event documents\ ✅ AI information extraction\ ✅ AI-generated case summaries\ ✅ Reviewer validation workflow\ ✅ Audit trail tracking

    Out of Scope

    ❌ Fully autonomous case approval\ ❌ Replacement of medical reviewers\ ❌ Regulatory submission automation

    5. Stakeholders

    Stakeholder Role

    Safety Director Business owner

    PV Reviewer Primary user

    Medical Reviewer Clinical validation

    IT Team System integration

    Regulatory Team Compliance

    6. Business Requirements

    BR-001

    The system should reduce manual case processing time.

    BR-002

    The system should support AI-assisted adverse event analysis.

    BR-003

    The system should maintain regulatory compliance requirements.

    7. Success Metrics

    Before:

    Average case processing:

    30 minutes

    Target:

    15 minutes

    Success:

    50% reduction in processing time.

    8. Assumptions

    • Historical case data is available

    • Users will participate in training

    • AI output requires human validation

    END OF BRD

    \<br>

    Document 2: FRD (Functional Requirement Document)

    Purpose of FRD

    FRD answers:

    WHAT should the system do?

    It converts business needs into system capabilities.

    Functional Requirement Document

    Feature:

    AI Adverse Event Summary Generator

    Functional Requirement FR-001

    Requirement Name

    Document Upload

    Description

    The system shall allow users to upload adverse event documents.

    Actor

    PV Reviewer

    Input

    PDF, DOCX reports

    Expected Output

    Document stored successfully.

    Acceptance Criteria

    Given:

    User is logged in

    When:

    User uploads a valid document

    Then:

    System should store the document and confirm upload.

    Functional Requirement FR-002

    Requirement Name

    AI Information Extraction

    Description

    The system shall extract relevant adverse event information from uploaded documents.

    Extracted Fields

    • Patient information

    • Drug information

    • Event description

    • Reporter information

    • Timeline

    Acceptance Criteria

    Given:

    A valid document exists

    When:

    Extraction starts

    Then:

    AI should display extracted information for review.

    Functional Requirement FR-003

    Requirement Name

    AI Summary Generation

    Description:

    The system shall generate a draft safety narrative.

    Input:

    Extracted case information.

    Output:

    AI-generated summary.

    Acceptance Criteria:

    Given:

    Extracted case data exists

    When:

    Reviewer clicks generate summary

    Then:

    System creates a draft summary.

    Functional Requirement FR-004

    Requirement Name

    Human Validation

    Description:

    Reviewer shall be able to edit and approve AI-generated content.

    Functional Requirement FR-005

    Requirement Name

    Audit Trail

    Description:

    System shall record:

    • User activity

    • AI suggestions

    • Changes made

    • Approval history

    Non Functional Requirements

    NFR-001 Performance

    System should generate summary within 10 seconds.

    NFR-002 Security

    Only authorized users can access patient information.

    NFR-003 Compliance

    System should maintain complete audit history.

    END OF FRD

    \<br>

    Document 3: User Stories (Agile Format)

    User stories are smaller requirements used in Agile teams.

    Epic:

    AI-Assisted Pharmacovigilance Case Processing

    Feature:

    AI Case Summary Generation

    User Story 1

    Title:

    Generate AI Case Summary

    Story:

    As a pharmacovigilance reviewer,\ I want the system to generate an AI-based case summary,\ so that I can reduce manual documentation effort.

    Acceptance Criteria

    Scenario 1:

    Given:

    A case document has been uploaded

    When:

    The reviewer clicks Generate Summary

    Then:

    The system should create an AI-generated summary

    User Story 2

    Title:

    Review AI Extracted Information

    Story:

    As a safety reviewer,\ I want to review extracted case information,\ so that I can correct errors before submission.

    Acceptance Criteria:

    Given:

    AI extracted patient information

    When:

    Reviewer opens the case

    Then:

    Extracted fields should be editable

    User Story 3

    Title:

    Track AI Changes

    Story:

    As a compliance manager,\ I want an audit trail of AI suggestions,\ so that regulatory requirements are satisfied.

    Acceptance Criteria:

    Given:

    AI generates a recommendation

    When:

    A user modifies the recommendation

    Then:

    The system records the change history

    Document 4: Requirement Traceability Matrix (RTM)

    This connects everything.

    Business Requirement Functional User Test Requirement Story Case

    Reduce processing time AI Summary US-001 TC-001

    Improve accuracy Data Extraction US-002 TC-002

    Compliance Audit Trail US-003 TC-003

    Document 5: Business Rules

    Rules that system must follow.

    Example:

    Rule BRULE-001

    AI output cannot be submitted without human approval.

    Rule BRULE-002

    Only authorized safety reviewers can approve cases.

    Rule BRULE-003

    All AI-generated content must be logged.

    PHASE 2 -- Business Analysis Techniques & Requirement Analysis

    Lesson 13: User Story Mastery for BA & Product Roles

    A user story is not just a sentence. It is a bridge between user needs, business value, and development work.

    In Agile organizations, BAs and Product Owners rarely hand over huge requirement documents to developers.

    Instead, requirements are converted into:

    • Epics

    • Features

    • User Stories

    • Acceptance Criteria

    • Tasks

    Learning Objectives

    By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

    • What is a User Story

    • User Story structure

    • Epic vs Feature vs User Story

    • INVEST principle

    • How to write good user stories

    • Story splitting techniques

    • Acceptance criteria patterns

    • Definition of Ready (DoR)

    • Definition of Done (DoD)

    • AI Product user stories

    1. What is a User Story?

    Definition:

    A User Story is a short description of a user\'s need written from the user\'s perspective, explaining what they want and why they need it.

    Basic format:

    As a \<user>

    I want \<capability>

    So that \<business value>

    Example:

    Weak requirement:

    \"Create AI summary feature.\"

    User Story:

    As a pharmacovigilance reviewer,\ I want AI-generated case summaries,\ so that I can reduce manual documentation time.

    2. Why User Stories Are Important

    Traditional approach:

    Business → Document → Developer

    Problem:

    • Too much documentation

    • Less collaboration

    • Misunderstanding

    Agile approach:

    User Need → Conversation → User Story → Solution

    Benefits:

    ✅ Focus on user value\ ✅ Better collaboration\ ✅ Easier prioritization\ ✅ Easier testing

    3. Epic vs Feature vs User Story

    Very important interview topic.

    Epic

    Large business capability.

    Too big for one sprint.

    Example:

    Epic:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Case Processing

    Contains:

    • Document upload

    • AI extraction

    • Summary generation

    • Review workflow

    • Reporting

    Feature

    A major capability inside an Epic.

    Example:

    Feature:

    AI Case Summary Generation

    User Story

    Small piece of functionality deliverable in a sprint.

    Example:

    User Story:

    As a reviewer, I want AI-generated summaries so that I can reduce review time.

    Hierarchy:

    EPIC

    ↓

    FEATURE

    ↓

    USER STORY

    ↓

    TASK

    Example:

    Epic:

    Online Banking

    ↓

    Feature:

    Fund Transfer

    ↓

    User Story:

    As a customer, I want to transfer money so that I can pay bills.

    ↓

    Tasks:

    • Create transfer API

    • Build UI

    • Add validation

    4. Anatomy of a Good User Story

    A complete user story has:

    1. Title

    Short description.

    Example:

    Generate AI Case Summary

    2. User Role

    Who needs it?

    Example:

    Safety Reviewer

    3. Goal

    What capability is needed?

    Example:

    Generate summary

    4. Business Value

    Why?

    Example:

    Reduce manual effort

    5. Acceptance Criteria

    How will we know it works?

    5. INVEST Principle

    A good user story follows INVEST.

    Created by Bill Wake.

    I -- Independent

    Story should not depend heavily on another story.

    Bad:

    \"Complete checkout after payment system is built.\"

    Good:

    \"Validate payment details.\"

    N -- Negotiable

    Story is a discussion point.

    Not a fixed contract.

    V -- Valuable

    Must provide user/business value.

    Bad:

    \"Create database table.\"

    Good:

    \"Store customer information securely.\"

    E -- Estimable

    Team should estimate effort.

    S -- Small

    Should fit within a sprint.

    T -- Testable

    Should have clear acceptance criteria.

    6. Writing Good User Stories

    A good user story answers:

    Who?

    User/persona

    What?

    Need/capability

    Why?

    Business value

    Example:

    Bad:

    System should generate reports.

    Better:

    As a regulatory manager, I want automated safety reports so that I can submit compliance documents faster.

    7. User Story Splitting Techniques

    Large stories often cannot fit into a sprint.

    BA must split them.

    Technique 1: Split by Workflow Step

    Large:

    \"Process adverse event case.\"

    Split:

    1. Upload document

    2. Extract information

    3. Review extracted data

    4. Approve case

    Technique 2: Split by User Role

    Large:

    \"Manage safety cases.\"

    Split:

    Reviewer:

    Review cases

    Manager:

    Approve cases

    Admin:

    Configure workflow

    Technique 3: Split by Data Type

    Large:

    \"Extract all medical information.\"

    Split:

    • Extract patient details

    • Extract drug details

    • Extract event details

    Technique 4: Split by Business Rule

    Large:

    \"Handle case approval.\"

    Split:

    • Approve normal cases

    • Escalate critical cases

    8. Acceptance Criteria

    Acceptance criteria define completion conditions.

    They answer:

    \"How will we verify this works?\"

    Example:

    User Story:

    \"As a reviewer, I want AI summary generation.\"

    Acceptance Criteria:

    Given:

    A valid adverse event document exists

    When:

    Reviewer selects Generate Summary

    Then:

    AI should create a draft summary

    And:

    Reviewer should be able to edit it

    9. Acceptance Criteria Types

    Functional Criteria

    Checks functionality.

    Example:

    \"System generates summary.\"

    Validation Criteria

    Checks correctness.

    Example:

    \"Required fields must be displayed.\"

    Error Criteria

    Checks failures.

    Example:

    \"If document format is invalid, show error message.\"

    Security Criteria

    Checks access.

    Example:

    \"Only authorized users can view patient data.\"

    10. Definition of Ready (DoR)

    Before development starts, story should be ready.

    A story is Ready when:

    ✅ Clear description\ ✅ Acceptance criteria written\ ✅ Dependencies identified\ ✅ Business value understood\ ✅ Estimated by team

    Example:

    Developer should not receive:

    \"Build AI feature.\"

    Developer should receive:

    Complete user story with:

    • Context

    • Criteria

    • Rules

    11. Definition of Done (DoD)

    Defines when work is completed.

    Example:

    Feature is Done when:

    ✅ Code completed\ ✅ Tested\ ✅ Reviewed\ ✅ Acceptance criteria passed\ ✅ Documentation updated\ ✅ Product Owner approval received

    DoR vs DoD

    DoR DoD

    Before development After development

    Is it ready? Is it complete?

    Entry criteria Exit criteria

    12. AI Product User Story Examples

    Epic:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

    Feature:

    AI Document Intelligence

    Story 1:

    As a PV reviewer, I want uploaded documents automatically analyzed so that I can avoid manual data entry.

    Acceptance Criteria:

    • PDF upload supported

    • Data extracted

    • Errors highlighted

    Story 2:

    As a medical reviewer, I want AI-generated summaries so that I can review cases faster.

    Acceptance Criteria:

    • Summary generated

    • Editable

    • Audit tracked

    Story 3:

    As a compliance manager, I want AI decisions logged so that regulatory audits can be supported.

    Acceptance Criteria:

    • All AI actions recorded

    • User activity tracked

    13. User Story vs Functional Requirement

    Interview favorite.

    Functional Requirement

    System focused.

    Example:

    \"The system shall generate case summaries.\"

    User Story

    User focused.

    Example:

    \"As a reviewer, I want summaries so that I save time.\"

    Relationship:

    Business Need

    ↓

    User Story

    ↓

    Functional Requirement

    ↓

    Development

    ↓

    Testing

    14. BA Interview Questions

    Q1: What is a user story?

    Answer:

    \"A user story is an Agile requirement format that describes a user\'s need, desired capability, and business value.\"

    Q2: Difference between Epic and User Story?

    Answer:

    \"An Epic represents a large capability that is broken into smaller user stories that can be completed within iterations.\"

    Q3: What is INVEST?

    Answer:

    \"INVEST is a guideline for writing effective user stories: Independent, Negotiable, Valuable, Estimable, Small, and Testable.\"

    Practical Assignment

    Create backlog hierarchy for:

    Product:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

    Create:

    2 Epics

    Example:

    • AI Case Processing

    • Regulatory Reporting

    For each Epic:

    Create:

    • 3 Features

    For each Feature:

    Create:

    • 2 User Stories

    For each User Story:

    Add:

    • Acceptance Criteria

    Lesson Summary

    You learned:

    ✅ User Stories\ ✅ Epic vs Feature vs Story\ ✅ INVEST principle\ ✅ Story splitting\ ✅ Acceptance Criteria\ ✅ Definition of Ready\ ✅ Definition of Done\ ✅ AI Product examples

    Lesson 14: Advanced Requirement Techniques

    Prioritization, MoSCoW, Kano Model, WSJF, Cost of Delay & Change Management

    A BA\'s job is not to collect every requirement. A BA\'s job is to identify which requirements create the highest business value.

    In real projects, stakeholders always say:

    • \"Everything is important.\"

    • \"This feature is critical.\"

    • \"We need this immediately.\"

    But resources are limited:

    • Limited budget

    • Limited developers

    • Limited time

    • Limited capacity

    A BA/Product Manager must answer:

    \"What should we build first?\"

    Learning Objectives

    By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

    • Requirement prioritization concept

    • MoSCoW technique

    • Kano Model

    • WSJF prioritization

    • Cost of Delay

    • Backlog refinement

    • Requirement Traceability Matrix (advanced)

    • Change management process

    1. What is Requirement Prioritization?

    Definition:

    Requirement prioritization is the process of ranking requirements based on business value, urgency, risk, cost, and stakeholder needs.

    Simple:

    Not all requirements are equal.

    Example:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Platform:

    Requirements:

    1. User login

    2. AI case summary

    3. Dashboard color theme

    4. Regulatory audit trail

    Which comes first?

    A BA evaluates:

    • Business value

    • Compliance importance

    • Risk

    • Effort

    2. Why Prioritization is Important

    Without prioritization:

    ❌ Team builds low-value features\ ❌ Important features delayed\ ❌ Budget increases\ ❌ Product loses focus

    With prioritization:

    ✅ Faster value delivery\ ✅ Better MVP decisions\ ✅ Better stakeholder alignment

    3. Requirement Prioritization Factors

    A BA considers:

    Business Value

    Does it solve an important business problem?

    Example:

    Reduce case processing time.

    Customer/User Value

    Does it improve user experience?

    Example:

    AI-generated summaries.

    Risk Reduction

    Does it reduce business risk?

    Example:

    Audit trail capability.

    Regulatory Need

    Is it mandatory?

    Example:

    Compliance reporting.

    Cost/Effort

    How difficult is implementation?

    Dependencies

    Does another feature need to exist first?

    4. MoSCoW Prioritization Technique

    One of the most popular BA techniques.

    MoSCoW:

    Category Meaning

    M Must Have

    S Should Have

    C Could Have

    W Won\'t Have

    M -- Must Have

    Critical requirements.

    Without them, solution fails.

    Example:

    AI Pharmacovigilance MVP:

    ✅ User authentication\ ✅ Case upload\ ✅ Security controls\ ✅ Human approval workflow

    S -- Should Have

    Important but not immediately critical.

    Example:

    • Advanced analytics dashboard

    • Export reports

    C -- Could Have

    Nice-to-have features.

    Example:

    • Custom themes

    • Additional charts

    W -- Won\'t Have (Now)

    Not planned in current release.

    Example:

    • Fully autonomous AI approval

    MoSCoW Example

    AI Safety Platform:

    Requirement Priority

    Upload case documents Must

    AI extraction Must

    AI prediction model Should

    Custom dashboard Could

    Fully automated approval Won\'t

    5. Kano Model

    Developed by:

    Noriaki Kano

    Used to understand customer satisfaction.

    Kano classifies features into:

    1. Basic Needs

    2. Performance Needs

    3. Excitement Needs

    1. Basic Needs (Must Have)

    Users expect them.

    Absence causes dissatisfaction.

    Example:

    Healthcare app:

    Secure login.

    2. Performance Needs

    More is better.

    Example:

    Faster AI response time.

    3. Excitement Needs

    Unexpected features that delight users.

    Example:

    AI proactively identifies safety risks.

    Kano Example

    AI Pharmacovigilance Product:

    Feature Kano Type

    Secure access Basic

    Faster processing Performance

    AI risk prediction Excitement

    6. WSJF (Weighted Shortest Job First)

    Very important in SAFe Agile.

    Used to prioritize work based on economic value.

    Formula:

    WSJF = Cost of Delay / Job Size

    Cost of Delay consists of:

    Business Value

    +

    Time Criticality

    +

    Risk Reduction

    +

    Opportunity Enablement

    Example:

    Feature A:

    AI Summary

    Cost of Delay = 100

    Effort = 10

    WSJF:

    100 / 10 = 10

    Feature B:

    Dashboard Theme

    Cost of Delay = 20

    Effort = 5

    WSJF:

    20 / 5 = 4

    Priority:

    Feature A first.

    7. Cost of Delay

    Question:

    \"How much value do we lose by delaying this feature?\"

    Example:

    Regulatory reporting feature.

    Delay:

    3 months.

    Impact:

    • Compliance risk

    • Financial penalty

    • Reputation damage

    High Cost of Delay.

    8. Backlog Refinement

    Also called:

    Backlog Grooming.

    Definition:

    A continuous activity where the team reviews, clarifies, estimates, and prioritizes backlog items.

    Participants:

    • Product Owner

    • BA

    • Developers

    • QA Team

    Activities:

    Remove ambiguity

    Example:

    \"Improve AI system.\"

    becomes:

    \"Reduce AI summary generation time below 5 seconds.\"

    Add acceptance criteria

    Split large stories

    Estimate effort

    Reprioritize

    9. Requirement Traceability Matrix (Advanced)

    RTM ensures every requirement is tracked.

    Basic flow:

    Business Goal

    ↓

    Requirement

    ↓

    User Story

    ↓

    Development Task

    ↓

    Test Case

    ↓

    Release

    Example:

    Business Goal Requirement Story Test

    Reduce processing time AI summary US-001 TC-001

    Improve compliance Audit log US-002 TC-002

    Benefits of RTM

    ✅ Prevent missing requirements\ ✅ Supports audits\ ✅ Helps impact analysis\ ✅ Controls changes

    10. Requirement Change Management

    Requirements change frequently.

    Examples:

    • New regulation

    • Customer request

    • Market change

    • Technical limitation

    A BA manages changes through:

    Step 1: Change Request

    Stakeholder submits request.

    Example:

    \"Add AI multilingual support.\"

    Step 2: Analyze Impact

    Evaluate:

    • Cost

    • Timeline

    • Scope

    • Risk

    • Dependencies

    Step 3: Decision

    Approve / Reject / Modify

    Step 4: Update Documents

    Update:

    • BRD

    • FRD

    • User Stories

    • RTM

    • Backlog

    Change Management Flow

    Change Request

    ↓

    Impact Analysis

    ↓

    Approval

    ↓

    Requirement Update

    ↓

    Development

    ↓

    Testing

    11. Healthcare AI Example

    Request:

    \"Add AI-generated regulatory reports.\"

    BA Analysis:

    Business Value:

    High

    Compliance Impact:

    High

    Effort:

    Medium

    Dependencies:

    Requires case data validation.

    Prioritization:

    MoSCoW:

    Must Have

    Reason:

    Regulatory importance.

    12. Interview Questions

    Q1: How do you prioritize requirements?

    Answer:

    \"I prioritize requirements using techniques such as MoSCoW, business value scoring, risk assessment, WSJF, and stakeholder alignment.\"

    Q2: Explain MoSCoW.

    Answer:

    \"MoSCoW categorizes requirements into Must Have, Should Have, Could Have, and Won\'t Have to help teams focus on the highest-value items.\"

    Q3: What is backlog refinement?

    Answer:

    \"Backlog refinement is the continuous process of reviewing, clarifying, splitting, estimating, and prioritizing backlog items before development.\"

    Practical Assignment

    For AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot:

    Create a prioritization table.

    Requirements:

    1. User Login

    2. Document Upload

    3. AI Extraction

    4. AI Summary

    5. Risk Prediction

    6. Dashboard Analytics

    7. Mobile App

    8. Automated Regulatory Submission

    Assign:

    MoSCoW Priority

    Business Value (1-5)

    Effort (1-5)

    Final Recommendation

    Lesson Summary

    You learned:

    ✅ Requirement prioritization\ ✅ MoSCoW\ ✅ Kano Model\ ✅ WSJF\ ✅ Cost of Delay\ ✅ Backlog Refinement\ ✅ Advanced RTM\ ✅ Change Management

    ✅ Lesson 10 -- Requirement Engineering Fundamentals\ ✅ Lesson 11 -- Requirement Elicitation\ ✅ Lesson 12 -- Requirement Analysis & Documentation\ ✅ Lesson 13 -- User Story Mastery\ ✅ Lesson 14 -- Advanced Requirement Techniques

    PHASE 3 -- Process Modeling & Solution Design

    Lesson 15: Process Modeling Fundamentals

    Understanding Processes, Workflows, AS-IS & TO-BE Analysis

    A BA does not only define what a system should do. A BA understands how the business works today and designs how it should work tomorrow.

    Before creating software requirements, a BA must understand the underlying business process.

    Example:

    A stakeholder says:

    \"We need AI automation for pharmacovigilance.\"

    A weak BA asks:

    \"What feature do you want?\"

    A strong BA asks:

    \"How does the current safety process work? Where are delays? Where can AI improve the workflow?\"

    Learning Objectives

    By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

    • What is a business process

    • Process vs workflow

    • Process modeling concepts

    • Why BAs model processes

    • AS-IS process mapping

    • TO-BE process design

    • Process improvement approach

    • Flowcharts

    • SIPOC Model

    • Value Stream Mapping

    • Healthcare process example

    1. What is a Business Process?

    Definition:

    A business process is a sequence of activities performed to achieve a specific business outcome.

    Simple:

    A process describes:

    Who does what, when, and why.

    Example:

    Pharmacovigilance Case Processing Process:

    Receive Case

    ↓

    Validate Information

    ↓

    Enter Data

    ↓

    Medical Review

    ↓

    Quality Check

    ↓

    Submit Report

    2. Process Components

    Every process has:

    1. Trigger

    What starts the process?

    Example:

    Adverse event report received.

    2. Inputs

    What information enters?

    Example:

    Patient report, medical documents.

    3. Activities

    Steps performed.

    Example:

    Review, classify, approve.

    4. Actors

    Who performs activities?

    Example:

    Safety reviewer.

    5. Outputs

    Final result.

    Example:

    Regulatory safety report.

    6. Rules

    Constraints.

    Example:

    Serious cases require escalation.

    Process Structure

    Trigger

    ↓

    Input

    ↓

    Activities

    ↓

    Decision Points

    ↓

    Output

    3. What is Process Modeling?

    Definition:

    Process modeling is the visual representation of business processes using diagrams, symbols, and rules.

    Instead of writing:

    \"Reviewer checks case, approves, then submits.\"

    We draw:

    Receive Case

    |

    Validate Data

    |

    Medical Review

    |

    Approval

    |

    Submission

    Why Process Modeling?

    A BA uses process models to:

    ✅ Understand current operations\ ✅ Identify problems\ ✅ Remove inefficiencies\ ✅ Define automation opportunities\ ✅ Communicate with stakeholders\ ✅ Design improved workflows

    4. Process vs Workflow

    This is a common interview question.

    Business Process

    Higher-level view.

    Focus:

    Business objective.

    Example:

    \"Manage Adverse Event Cases.\"

    Workflow

    Detailed sequence of tasks.

    Focus:

    How work moves between people/systems.

    Example:

    Reviewer receives case

    ↓

    AI extracts information

    ↓

    Reviewer validates

    ↓

    Manager approves

    Difference:

    Process Workflow

    Business view Operational view

    Why work happens How work happens

    Strategic Detailed

    End-to-end Task sequence

    5. Process Modeling Levels

    A BA usually works at different levels.

    Level 0: Enterprise Process Map

    High-level business areas.

    Example:

    Pharmaceutical Company:

    Drug Development

    ↓

    Clinical Trials

    ↓

    Regulatory Affairs

    ↓

    Pharmacovigilance

    ↓

    Product Support

    Level 1: Process

    Example:

    Pharmacovigilance Management.

    Level 2: Sub-process

    Example:

    Adverse Event Case Processing.

    Level 3: Activities

    Example:

    • Receive case

    • Validate data

    • Review case

    6. AS-IS Process Mapping

    What is AS-IS?

    AS-IS represents:

    How the process works today.

    Before improvement.

    Example:

    Current manual PV process:

    Report Received

    ↓

    Reviewer Reads Document

    ↓

    Manual Data Entry

    ↓

    Manual Classification

    ↓

    Medical Review

    ↓

    Submission

    Why Create AS-IS?

    To identify:

    • Bottlenecks

    • Delays

    • Manual work

    • Errors

    • Duplicate activities

    AS-IS Analysis Example

    Problem:

    Case processing takes 5 days.

    BA investigates:

    Activity Time

    Document reading 2 hours

    Data entry 3 hours

    Validation 1 day

    Approval waiting 2 days

    Finding:

    Approval waiting is biggest bottleneck.

    7. TO-BE Process Design

    What is TO-BE?

    TO-BE represents:

    How the future improved process should work.

    Example:

    AI-enabled PV process:

    Report Received

    ↓

    AI Extracts Information

    ↓

    AI Suggests Classification

    ↓

    Reviewer Validates

    ↓

    Medical Approval

    ↓

    Submission

    AS-IS vs TO-BE

    AS-IS TO-BE

    Manual extraction AI extraction

    Multiple systems Integrated platform

    Long review time Faster review

    More errors Validation support

    8. Process Improvement Cycle

    A BA follows:

    Understand Current Process

    ↓

    Identify Problems

    ↓

    Analyze Root Cause

    ↓

    Design Future Process

    ↓

    Define Requirements

    ↓

    Implement Solution

    ↓

    Measure Improvement

    9. Flowcharts

    One of the simplest process modeling techniques.

    Common symbols:

    Start / End

    Oval

    (Start)

    Activity

    Rectangle

    [Review Case]

    Decision

    Diamond

    \<Is Case Serious?>

    Flow

    Arrow

    ---->

    Example:

    (Start)

    ↓

    Receive Report

    ↓

    Validate Data

    ↓

    \<Complete?>

    Yes ↓

    Review Case

    No ↓

    Request Information

    10. SIPOC Model

    Very useful BA technique.

    SIPOC:

    Letter Meaning

    S Supplier

    I Input

    P Process

    O Output

    C Customer

    Example:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Process:

    SIPOC Example

    Supplier Healthcare provider

    Input Adverse event report

    Process Case processing

    Output Safety report

    Customer Regulatory authority

    When to use SIPOC?

    Use when:

    • Starting process analysis

    • Understanding scope

    • Identifying stakeholders

    11. Value Stream Mapping

    Used in Lean methodology.

    Purpose:

    Identify:

    • Value-added activities

    • Waste

    • Delays

    Example:

    Current process:

    Receive Report

    (Waiting 2 days)

    Manual Entry

    (Waiting 1 day)

    Review

    Waste:

    Waiting time.

    Future:

    AI reduces waiting.

    12. Healthcare AI Example

    Project:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

    AS-IS Process

    Patient Report

    ↓

    Safety Team Receives Document

    ↓

    Reviewer Reads Document

    ↓

    Manual Data Entry

    ↓

    Medical Review

    ↓

    Submission

    Problems:

    • Slow

    • Manual effort

    • Data errors

    TO-BE Process

    Patient Report

    ↓

    AI Document Processing

    ↓

    Information Extraction

    ↓

    Reviewer Validation

    ↓

    AI Summary Generation

    ↓

    Medical Approval

    ↓

    Submission

    Benefits:

    • Faster processing

    • Better consistency

    • Reduced workload

    13. Process Modeling Documents Created by BA

    Common artifacts:

    Document Purpose

    Process Map Understand workflow

    AS-IS Diagram Current state

    TO-BE Diagram Future state

    SIPOC Scope understanding

    Workflow Diagram Task sequence

    BPMN Model Detailed process

    14. Interview Questions

    Q1: Why do BAs create process models?

    Answer:

    \"Process models help BAs understand current operations, identify improvement opportunities, communicate with stakeholders, and define future-state solutions.\"

    Q2: Difference between AS-IS and TO-BE?

    Answer:

    \"AS-IS represents the current process, while TO-BE represents the improved future process after changes are implemented.\"

    Q3: What is SIPOC?

    Answer:

    \"SIPOC is a high-level process analysis technique that identifies Suppliers, Inputs, Process, Outputs, and Customers.\"

    Practical Assignment

    Create:

    AS-IS Process Map

    For:

    \"Current Pharmacovigilance Case Processing\"

    Include:

    • Trigger

    • Actors

    • Activities

    • Problems

    TO-BE Process Map

    After implementing:

    \"AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot\"

    Include:

    • AI activities

    • Human activities

    • Decision points

    • Improvements

    You learned:

    ✅ Business process concepts\ ✅ Process modeling\ ✅ Process vs workflow\ ✅ AS-IS mapping\ ✅ TO-BE design\ ✅ Flowcharts\ ✅ SIPOC\ ✅ Value Stream Mapping\ ✅ Healthcare AI process example

    PHASE 3 -- Process Modeling & Solution Design

    Lesson 16: BPMN 2.0 Fundamentals

    Business Process Model and Notation for Professional BAs

    BPMN is the language that allows business people, BAs, developers, architects, and process teams to understand the same business process visually.

    A senior BA is expected to convert business workflows into standardized process diagrams.

    Learning Objectives

    By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

    • What BPMN is

    • Why BAs use BPMN

    • BPMN vs Flowcharts

    • Core BPMN elements

    • Events

    • Activities

    • Gateways

    • Pools and Lanes

    • Sequence Flow

    • Message Flow

    • Basic BPMN modeling rules

    • Healthcare AI BPMN example

    1. What is BPMN?

    BPMN stands for:

    Business Process Model and Notation

    Definition:

    BPMN is a standardized graphical notation used to represent business processes and workflows.

    The current widely used standard is managed by the Object Management Group.

    Simple meaning:

    BPMN is a visual language for showing:

    • Who performs an activity

    • What happens next

    • Where decisions occur

    • How systems communicate

    2. Why BAs Use BPMN

    A BA works between:

    • Business stakeholders

    • Product teams

    • Developers

    • QA teams

    • Compliance teams

    Each group thinks differently.

    BPMN creates a common language.

    Example:

    Business says:

    \"The reviewer checks cases and sends approval.\"

    Developer needs:

    • Who checks?

    • When?

    • What conditions?

    • What happens if rejected?

    BPMN answers these questions.

    3. BPMN vs Flowchart

    Both represent processes, but BPMN is more powerful.

    Flowchart BPMN

    Simple diagrams Industry standard

    Basic steps Detailed business logic

    Limited symbols Rich notation

    Small processes Enterprise processes

    Example:

    Flowchart:

    Receive Case

    |

    Review

    |

    Approve

    BPMN:

    Patient → System → Reviewer → Manager → Regulatory Team

    with:

    - events

    - decisions

    - messages

    - responsibilities

    4. BPMN Core Elements

    BPMN has four main categories:

    1. Flow Objects

    2. Connecting Objects

    3. Swimlanes

    4. Artifacts

    1. Flow Objects

    These represent what happens in a process.

    Three main types:

    1. Events

    2. Activities

    3. Gateways

    5. Events

    Events represent something that happens.

    Usually drawn as circles.

    There are three types:

    A. Start Event

    Shows where the process begins.

    Symbol:

    ○

    Example:

    \"Adverse Event Report Received\"

    B. Intermediate Event

    Something happens during the process.

    Example:

    \"Timer Waiting\"

    \"Approval Received\"

    C. End Event

    Shows process completion.

    Symbol:

    ◎

    Example:

    \"Safety Report Submitted\"

    BPMN Event Flow

    (Start)

    ↓

    Process Activities

    ↓

    (End)

    6. Activities

    Activities represent work performed.

    Symbol:

    Rounded rectangle.

    [ Activity ]

    Two types:

    Task

    Single activity.

    Example:

    [Review Case]

    Sub-Process

    A group of activities.

    Example:

    [Process Safety Case]

    contains:

    - Extract Data

    - Validate Data

    - Classify Event

    7. Gateways

    Gateways represent decisions.

    Symbol:

    Diamond.

    ◇

    Example:

    Decision:

    \"Is case serious?\"

    ◇

    Serious?

    / \

    Yes No

    Types of Gateways

    1. Exclusive Gateway (XOR)

    Only one path selected.

    Example:

    If serious:

    Escalate

    Else:

    Normal processing

    Symbol:

    ◇ with X

    2. Parallel Gateway (AND)

    Multiple paths happen together.

    Example:

    After case validation:

    • Medical review

    • Data quality check

    Both happen.

    Symbol:

    ◇ with +

    3. Inclusive Gateway (OR)

    One or more paths possible.

    8. Connecting Objects

    They show relationships.

    Sequence Flow

    Shows order of activities.

    Symbol:

    Solid arrow.

    Example:

    Task A → Task B

    Used inside the same process.

    Message Flow

    Shows communication between different participants.

    Symbol:

    Dashed arrow.

    Example:

    Pharma Company sends report to Regulatory Authority.

    Company

    |

    | - - - >

    |

    Regulator

    Association

    Connects notes or information.

    9. Swimlanes: Pools and Lanes

    Very important BPMN concept.

    Used to show responsibility.

    Pool

    Represents a participant.

    Example:

    A company.

    -------------------

    Pharma Company

    -------------------

    Lane

    Represents roles/departments inside a pool.

    Example:

    --------------------------------

    Pharma Company

    Safety Reviewer

    Medical Reviewer

    Regulatory Team

    --------------------------------

    Example:

    AI Pharmacovigilance BPMN Participants:

    Pool:

    Pharmaceutical Company

    Lanes:

    1. Safety Reviewer

    2. AI System

    3. Medical Reviewer

    4. Regulatory Team

    10. BPMN Modeling Rules

    Important rules:

    Rule 1

    Every process needs:

    Start Event

    and

    End Event

    Rule 2

    Sequence flow connects activities.

    Rule 3

    Use gateways for decisions.

    Rule 4

    Use lanes to show responsibility.

    Rule 5

    Do not mix business process and technical details unnecessarily.

    11. Basic BPMN Example

    Process:

    AI Adverse Event Processing

    START

    ↓

    [Receive Report]

    ↓

    [AI Extract Information]

    ↓

    ◇ Complete Data?

    ↓Yes

    [Reviewer Validation]

    ↓

    ◇ Serious Case?

    ↓Yes

    [Medical Review]

    ↓

    [Regulatory Submission]

    ↓

    END

    12. Healthcare AI BPMN Design

    Pool:

    Pharmaceutical Company

    Lane 1:

    Safety Reviewer

    Activities:

    • Receive case

    • Validate AI output

    Lane 2:

    AI System

    Activities:

    • Extract information

    • Generate summary

    Lane 3:

    Medical Reviewer

    Activities:

    • Clinical assessment

    Lane 4:

    Regulatory Team

    Activities:

    • Submit report

    Visual logic:

    Safety Reviewer

    (Start)

    |

    Receive Case

    |

    AI System

    |

    Extract Data

    |

    Generate Summary

    |

    Safety Reviewer

    |

    Validate

    |

    Medical Reviewer

    |

    Approve

    |

    Regulatory

    |

    Submit

    (End)

    13. BPMN Tools Used by BAs

    Common tools:

    • Camunda Modeler

    • Signavio

    • Microsoft Visio

    • Lucid Software

    14. Interview Questions

    Q1: What is BPMN?

    Answer:

    \"BPMN is a standardized notation used to visually model business processes, showing activities, events, decisions, responsibilities, and communication flows.\"

    Q2: Difference between BPMN and Flowchart?

    Answer:

    \"Flowcharts provide simple process visualization, while BPMN provides a standardized enterprise-level notation with concepts like events, gateways, pools, and message flows.\"

    Q3: What are BPMN swimlanes?

    Answer:

    \"Swimlanes, consisting of pools and lanes, represent participants and responsibilities involved in a process.\"

    Practical Assignment

    Create a BPMN Level-1 process for:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Case Processing

    Include:

    Pool:

    Pharmaceutical Company

    Lanes:

    1. Safety Reviewer

    2. AI System

    3. Medical Reviewer

    4. Regulatory Team

    Include:

    • Start Event

    • End Event

    • Minimum 8 Activities

    • One Exclusive Gateway

    • One Message Flow
