    You learned:

    ✅ BPMN purpose\ ✅ BPMN vs Flowcharts\ ✅ Events\ ✅ Activities\ ✅ Gateways\ ✅ Pools and Lanes\ ✅ Sequence Flow\ ✅ Message Flow\ ✅ BPMN modeling rules\ ✅ Healthcare AI example

    Lesson 17: Advanced BPMN Modeling

    From Basic Workflow to Enterprise-Level Process Design

    Basic BPMN shows what happens. Advanced BPMN explains exceptions, timing, failures, parallel activities, and complex business decisions.

    In real organizations, processes are rarely simple.

    Example:

    A pharmacovigilance process is not:

    Receive Case → Review → Submit

    Real life:

    • What if documents are incomplete?

    • What if reviewer rejects the case?

    • What if approval is delayed?

    • What if AI extraction fails?

    • What if multiple teams work simultaneously?

    Advanced BPMN handles these situations.

    Learning Objectives

    By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

    • Intermediate Events

    • Timer Events

    • Error Events

    • Boundary Events

    • Message Events

    • Parallel Processing

    • Event-Based Gateway

    • Sub-Processes

    • Call Activities

    • BPMN best practices

    • Enterprise healthcare AI workflow

    1. BPMN Advanced Elements Overview

    Basic BPMN:

    Start

    |

    Task

    |

    Decision

    |

    End

    Advanced BPMN adds:

    Events

    ↓

    Exceptions

    ↓

    Parallel activities

    ↓

    Reusable processes

    ↓

    System communication

    2. Intermediate Events

    What are Intermediate Events?

    An event that occurs between start and end of a process.

    It represents something happening during execution.

    Example:

    Pharmacovigilance workflow:

    Start

    ↓

    Receive Case

    ↓

    (wait for additional documents)

    ↓

    Review Case

    ↓

    End

    The waiting step is an intermediate event.

    Types of Intermediate Events

    1. Timer Event

    2. Message Event

    3. Error Event

    4. Signal Event

    3. Timer Events

    A timer event represents time-based conditions.

    Symbol:

    Circle with clock.

    Example:

    Regulatory rule:

    \"Serious adverse events must be submitted within 15 days.\"

    BPMN:

    Case Approved

    ↓

    Timer Event

    (15 Days)

    ↓

    Submit Report

    Use cases:

    • SLA deadlines

    • Reminders

    • Escalations

    • Scheduled activities

    Healthcare Example:

    If medical reviewer does not approve within 24 hours:

    System sends reminder.

    Process:

    Waiting for Approval

    ↓

    Timer (24 hours)

    ↓

    Send Reminder

    4. Message Events

    Used when communication happens between participants.

    Example:

    Pharmaceutical Company sends report to Regulatory Authority.

    BPMN:

    Company Pool

    [Prepare Report]

    |

    | - - - - >

    |

    Regulatory Authority Pool

    [Receive Report]

    Important:

    Sequence Flow:

    Inside same process.

    Message Flow:

    Between different participants.

    5. Error Events

    Used when something goes wrong.

    Example:

    AI extraction fails.

    Normal flow:

    Upload Document

    ↓

    AI Extraction

    ↓

    Generate Summary

    Error scenario:

    Upload Document

    ↓

    AI Extraction

    X

    Extraction Failed

    ↓

    Manual Review

    6. Boundary Events

    Very important BPMN concept.

    A boundary event is attached to an activity.

    It represents an exception during that activity.

    Example:

    Activity:

    \"AI Processing\"

    Possible problem:

    Processing timeout.

    BPMN:

    +----------------+

    | AI Processing |

    | |

    | ◯ Timer |

    +----------------+

    ↓

    Manual Review

    Meaning:

    If AI takes too long:

    Move to manual review.

    Boundary Event Types

    Interrupting Boundary Event

    Stops current activity.

    Example:

    AI failure → Manual process.

    Non-interrupting Boundary Event

    Allows activity to continue.

    Example:

    AI processing continues while notification is sent.

    7. Parallel Gateway

    Used when multiple activities happen simultaneously.

    Symbol:

    Diamond with +

    Example:

    After case validation:

    Two teams work together:

    • Medical Review

    • Data Quality Check

    Process:

    +

    |

    ----------------

    | |

    Medical Review Data Check

    | |

    ----------------

    +

    Continue

    Healthcare Example:

    Case review requires:

    Medical assessment + Regulatory verification.

    Both happen together.

    8. Event-Based Gateway

    Used when the next action depends on an event.

    Symbol:

    Diamond with event marker.

    Example:

    After submitting approval request:

    Possible outcomes:

    1. Manager approves

    2. Manager rejects

    3. Timeout occurs

    Process:

    ◇

    Approval Waiting

    / | \

    Approve Reject Timeout

    9. Sub-Processes

    A sub-process groups multiple activities.

    Why?

    To simplify large diagrams.

    Example:

    Instead of:

    Case Processing

    - Extract data

    - Validate fields

    - Check duplicates

    - Classify event

    - Generate narrative

    Create:

    [AI Case Processing Sub-process]

    Inside:

    Upload

    ↓

    Extract

    ↓

    Validate

    ↓

    Classify

    ↓

    Summarize

    10. Call Activity

    Used when a reusable process is called from multiple places.

    Example:

    \"User Authentication\"

    Used in:

    • Case Review

    • Reporting

    • Dashboard

    Instead of creating again:

    Call existing process.

    Sub-process vs Call Activity

    Sub-process Call Activity

    Local process Reusable process

    Used once Used multiple times

    Inside same workflow Shared across workflows

    11. Complete AI Pharmacovigilance BPMN Level-2 Example

    Pool:

    Pharmaceutical Company

    Lane: Safety Reviewer

    (Start)

    ↓

    Receive Case

    ↓

    Upload Document

    Lane: AI System

    AI Extraction

    ↓

    AI Classification

    ↓

    AI Summary Generation

    Decision:

    Is AI Confidence > 90%?

    YES:

    Reviewer Validation

    NO:

    Manual Review

    Parallel:

    +

    |

    Medical Review

    Regulatory Check

    |

    +

    Timer:

    Approval pending

    ↓

    24 hour timer

    ↓

    Escalation

    End:

    Report Submitted

    (End)

    12. BPMN Best Practices

    Practice 1:

    Always start with business objective.

    Do not immediately draw technical workflow.

    Practice 2:

    Keep diagrams readable.

    Avoid:

    • Too many crossings

    • Too many gateways

    Practice 3:

    Use proper naming.

    Bad:

    \"Process Data\"

    Good:

    \"Extract Patient Information\"

    Practice 4:

    Separate:

    Business activities

    from

    System activities.

    Practice 5:

    Model exceptions.

    Real processes include failures.

    13. BPMN in BA Interviews

    Q1: What is a boundary event?

    Answer:

    \"A boundary event is an event attached to an activity that handles exceptions or additional triggers while the activity is running.\"

    Q2: Difference between sequence flow and message flow?

    Answer:

    \"Sequence flow shows order of activities inside a process, while message flow represents communication between different participants.\"

    Q3: Why use subprocesses?

    Answer:

    \"Subprocesses simplify complex processes by grouping related activities into manageable sections.\"

    Practical Assignment

    Design Level-2 BPMN for:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

    Include:

    Events:

    ✅ Start Event\ ✅ Timer Event\ ✅ Error Event

    Gateways:

    ✅ Exclusive Gateway\ ✅ Parallel Gateway

    BPMN Structure:

    Pool:

    Pharmaceutical Company

    Lanes:

    1. Safety Reviewer

    2. AI Engine

    3. Medical Reviewer

    4. Regulatory Team

    Lesson Summary

    You learned:

    ✅ Intermediate Events\ ✅ Timer Events\ ✅ Message Events\ ✅ Error Events\ ✅ Boundary Events\ ✅ Parallel Gateway\ ✅ Event-Based Gateway\ ✅ Sub-processes\ ✅ Call Activities\ ✅ BPMN best practices\ ✅ Enterprise workflow modeling

    Lesson 18: UML for Business Analysts

    Understanding System Behavior Through UML Diagrams

    BPMN explains how the business works. UML explains how the system behaves.

    A professional BA often works between business processes and software solutions.

    Example:

    Business says:

    \"We need an AI pharmacovigilance platform.\"

    BPMN answers:

    \"How does the safety workflow operate?\"

    UML answers:

    \"How should users interact with the system?\"

    Learning Objectives

    By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

    • What UML is

    • Why BAs use UML

    • BPMN vs UML

    • UML diagram categories

    • Use Case Diagram

    • Activity Diagram

    • Sequence Diagram

    • Class Diagram basics

    • State Diagram

    • Healthcare AI examples

    1. What is UML?

    UML stands for:

    Unified Modeling Language

    Definition:

    UML is a standardized visual modeling language used to describe software systems, their structure, behavior, and interactions.

    Simple meaning:

    UML helps describe:

    • Users

    • System functions

    • Data objects

    • System interactions

    • Object behavior

    2. Why Does a BA Need UML?

    A BA does not usually write code.

    But a BA must communicate with:

    • Developers

    • Architects

    • QA Engineers

    • Product Teams

    UML helps translate:

    Business requirement

    ↓

    System understanding

    ↓

    Development solution

    Example:

    Business Requirement:

    \"Reviewer should approve AI-generated case summaries.\"

    Developer needs:

    • Who approves?

    • What happens before approval?

    • What data is involved?

    • What system response occurs?

    UML helps define this.

    3. BPMN vs UML

    Very important interview question.

    BPMN UML

    Business process modeling Software/system modeling

    Focus on workflow Focus on system behavior

    Shows activities Shows objects and interactions

    Business users understand Technical teams understand

    Example:

    BPMN:

    \"How a safety case moves through the organization.\"

    UML:

    \"How reviewer interacts with AI system.\"

    4. UML Diagram Categories

    UML has many diagrams.

    For BAs, the most important are:

    Behavioral Diagrams

    Show what the system does.

    Examples:

    1. Use Case Diagram

    2. Activity Diagram

    3. Sequence Diagram

    4. State Diagram

    Structural Diagrams

    Show system structure.

    Examples:

    1. Class Diagram

    2. Component Diagram

    BA Most Important UML Diagrams

    Diagram Purpose

    Use Case User-system interaction

    Activity Workflow behavior

    Sequence Communication between objects

    Class Data structure

    State Object lifecycle

    5. Use Case Diagram

    Most important UML diagram for BA.

    Definition:

    A Use Case Diagram shows how users interact with a system and what functionality the system provides.

    It contains:

    Actor

    Who uses the system.

    Example:

    • Safety Reviewer

    • Medical Reviewer

    • Administrator

    Use Case

    System functionality.

    Example:

    • Upload Document

    • Generate Summary

    • Approve Case

    Use Case Diagram Example

    System:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

    Safety Reviewer

    |

    |

    -----------------

    | AI PV System |

    -----------------

    |

    --------------------

    | |

    Upload Document Generate Summary

    |

    Approve Case

    Actors:

    External users or systems.

    Examples:

    • Reviewer

    • Regulatory Authority

    • Drug Database

    Use Cases:

    Actions performed by system.

    Examples:

    • Login

    • Upload case

    • Review AI output

    • Submit report

    6. Use Case Relationship

    Include Relationship

    Means:

    One use case always uses another.

    Example:

    Login required before:

    • Review Case

    • Submit Report

    Review Case

    |

    \<\<include>>

    |

    Login

    Extend Relationship

    Optional behavior.

    Example:

    Normal review:

    Additional:

    AI suggestion warning

    Review Case

    |

    \<\<extend>>

    |

    AI Warning

    7. Writing Use Cases

    A BA creates detailed use case specifications.

    Example:

    Use Case: Generate AI Summary

    Use Case ID

    UC-001

    Actor

    Safety Reviewer

    Goal

    Generate case summary using AI.

    Preconditions

    • User logged in

    • Document uploaded

    Main Flow

    1. User selects Generate Summary

    2. System extracts information

    3. AI generates summary

    4. User reviews output

    Alternative Flow

    AI confidence is low:

    System requests manual review.

    Post Condition

    Summary stored.

    8. Activity Diagram

    Activity Diagram represents workflow behavior.

    Similar to BPMN but UML style.

    Example:

    AI Summary Generation:

    Start

    ↓

    Upload Document

    ↓

    Extract Data

    ↓

    Generate Summary

    ↓

    Review

    ↓

    Approve

    ↓

    End

    Used for:

    • Business workflows

    • Algorithm flows

    • User actions

    BPMN Activity vs UML Activity Diagram

    They look similar.

    Difference:

    BPMN:

    Business process focus.

    UML Activity:

    System behavior focus.

    9. Sequence Diagram

    Very important for BA.

    Definition:

    A sequence diagram shows how different objects or systems communicate over time.

    Example:

    AI Summary Generation:

    Objects:

    1. Reviewer

    2. Application

    3. AI Engine

    4. Database

    Sequence:

    Reviewer

    |

    | Request Summary

    ↓

    Application

    |

    | Send Document

    ↓

    AI Engine

    |

    | Extract Data

    ↓

    Database

    |

    | Return Result

    ↓

    Application

    |

    | Display Summary

    ↓

    Reviewer

    Sequence diagrams answer:

    • Who talks to whom?

    • In what order?

    • What data moves?

    10. Class Diagram Basics

    Class Diagram shows data structure.

    A class contains:

    • Attributes

    • Methods

    Example:

    Class:

    Patient Case

    -------------------

    PatientCase

    Attributes:

    caseID

    patientAge

    drugName

    eventType

    Methods:

    validate()

    submit()

    -------------------

    Healthcare AI Example:

    Classes:

    • Patient

    • Drug

    • AdverseEvent

    • CaseReport

    • Reviewer

    11. State Diagram

    Shows how an object changes state.

    Example:

    Adverse Event Case Lifecycle:

    New

    ↓

    Under Review

    ↓

    Validated

    ↓

    Approved

    ↓

    Submitted

    Possible rejection:

    Under Review

    ↓

    Rejected

    ↓

    Rework

    Used for:

    • Workflow states

    • Status transitions

    • Approval processes

    12. Complete Healthcare AI Example

    Requirement:

    \"Reviewer should process AI-assisted safety cases.\"

    BPMN:

    Shows:

    Case received → AI processing → Review → Approval

    Use Case Diagram:

    Shows:

    Reviewer uses:

    • Upload Case

    • Generate Summary

    • Approve Case

    Sequence Diagram:

    Shows:

    Reviewer → System → AI → Database

    State Diagram:

    Shows:

    Case status:

    New → Review → Approved

    Together:

    Business Process

    ↓

    System Requirements

    ↓

    UML Models

    ↓

    Development

    13. UML Tools Used by BAs

    Common tools:

    • Lucid Software

    • Microsoft Visio

    • Visual Paradigm

    • Sparx Systems

    14. Interview Questions

    Q1: Why does a BA use UML?

    Answer:

    \"UML helps BAs visualize system behavior, clarify requirements, improve communication with technical teams, and reduce ambiguity.\"

    Q2: Difference between BPMN and UML?

    Answer:

    \"BPMN focuses on business processes and workflows, while UML focuses on software system structure and behavior.\"

    Q3: What is a Use Case Diagram?

    Answer:

    \"A Use Case Diagram represents interactions between actors and system functionalities.\"

    Practical Assignment

    \"Adverse Event Case\"

    States:

    • New

    You learned:

    ✅ UML purpose\ ✅ BPMN vs UML\ ✅ UML diagram types\ ✅ Use Case Diagram\ ✅ Activity Diagram\ ✅ Sequence Diagram\ ✅ Class Diagram\ ✅ State Diagram\ ✅ Healthcare AI application

    PHASE 3 -- Process Modeling & Solution Design

    Lesson 19: Workflow Design & User Journey Mapping

    Connecting Business Process, User Experience, and Product Design

    A BA does not only design how the company works. A BA also understands how users experience the product.

    A successful product requires both:

    1. Efficient internal workflow

    2. Excellent user experience

    Example:

    A pharmaceutical company may have a perfect internal process, but if safety reviewers find the AI platform difficult to use, adoption will fail.

    Learning Objectives

    By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

    • Difference between Process, Workflow, User Flow, and User Journey

    • Workflow design principles

    • End-to-end workflow modeling

    • User journey mapping

    • Customer Journey vs User Journey

    • Personas

    • Touchpoints

    • Pain points

    • Opportunities

    • Healthcare AI examples

    1. Process vs Workflow vs User Flow vs User Journey

    This is a very important BA/Product interview topic.

    1. Business Process

    Question:

    Why does the business perform this activity?

    Focus:

    Business objective.

    Example:

    \"Pharmacovigilance Case Management\"

    2. Workflow

    Question:

    How does work move between people and systems?

    Focus:

    Operational steps.

    Example:

    Case Received

    ↓

    AI Processing

    ↓

    Reviewer Validation

    ↓

    Medical Approval

    ↓

    Submission

    3. User Flow

    Question:

    What steps does a user take inside the product?

    Focus:

    User interaction with software.

    Example:

    Reviewer:

    Login

    ↓

    Open Dashboard

    ↓

    Select Case

    ↓

    Review AI Summary

    ↓

    Approve

    4. User Journey

    Question:

    What is the complete user experience before, during, and after using the product?

    Focus:

    User emotions, goals, problems.

    Example:

    Reviewer journey:

    Receives case

    ↓

    Feels overloaded

    ↓

    Uses AI tool

    ↓

    Reviews summary

    ↓

    Feels confident

    Comparison Table

    Concept Focus Example

    Process Business goal Case management

    Workflow Work movement Review approval

    User Flow Product interaction Screen navigation

    User Journey Experience User feelings

    2. What is Workflow Design?

    Definition:

    Workflow design is the process of designing the sequence of activities, decisions, responsibilities, and system interactions required to complete a task.

    A BA designs workflows to answer:

    • Who performs the task?

    • What happens next?

    • What decisions exist?

    • Where can automation help?

    • Where are bottlenecks?

    3. Workflow Design Principles

    Principle 1: Clear Ownership

    Every activity should have an owner.

    Bad:

    \"Review case\"

    Who reviews?

    Better:

    \"Safety reviewer validates extracted data.\"

    Principle 2: Minimize Manual Work

    Identify automation opportunities.

    Example:

    Manual:

    Copy patient details.

    Improvement:

    AI extraction.

    Principle 3: Design Exception Handling

    Real processes fail.

    Example:

    Missing information.

    Workflow should define:

    \"If data missing → request additional information.\"

    Principle 4: Reduce Unnecessary Steps

    Example:

    Old:

    System A

    ↓

    Manual Copy

    ↓

    System B

    Better:

    System Integration

    4. Workflow Design Example

    AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

    Current Workflow

    Receive Report

    ↓

    Reviewer Reads Document

    ↓

    Manual Data Entry

    ↓

    Medical Review

    ↓

    Approval

    ↓

    Submission

    Problems:

    ❌ Slow\ ❌ Manual effort\ ❌ Human errors

    Future Workflow

    Receive Report

    ↓

    AI Extracts Information

    ↓

    AI Generates Summary

    ↓

    Reviewer Validates

    ↓

    Medical Review

    ↓

    Submission

    Improvements:

    ✅ Faster processing\ ✅ Better consistency\ ✅ Reduced workload

    5. What is User Journey Mapping?

    Definition:

    User Journey Mapping is a visual representation of a user\'s experience while achieving a goal using a product or service.

    A journey map shows:

    • User actions

    • Thoughts

    • Feelings

    • Problems

    • Opportunities

    6. Components of User Journey Map

    A typical journey map contains:

    1. Persona

    Who is the user?

    Example:

    Persona:

    Name:

    Dr. Sharma

    Role:

    Pharmacovigilance Reviewer

    Goal:

    Process safety cases faster.

    2. Scenario

    What situation?

    Example:

    Reviewing adverse event reports.

    3. Stages

    Major journey steps.

    Example:

    1. Receive Case

    2. Analyze Information

    3. Review AI Output

    4. Approve

    5. Submit

    4. Actions

    What user does.

    5. Thoughts

    What user thinks.

    Example:

    \"Is this AI suggestion accurate?\"

    6. Emotions

    How user feels.

    Example:

    Before AI:

    😟 Overwhelmed

    After AI:

    😊 Confident

    7. Pain Points

    Problems.

    Example:

    • Too much manual reading

    • Missing information

    • Multiple systems

    8. Opportunities

    Possible improvements.

    Example:

    • AI summary

    • Smart alerts

    • Auto extraction

    7. User Journey Map Example

    Persona:

    Safety Reviewer

    Goal:

    Complete adverse event review.

    Stage Action Pain Point Opportunity

    Receive Case Opens report Too many cases AI prioritization

    Read Documents Manually extracts Time consuming AI extraction data

    Review Checks information Missing AI validation details

    Approval Completes review Waiting time Workflow automation

    8. Customer Journey vs User Journey

    Another important interview question.

    Customer Journey

    Broader experience with company.

    Example:

    Patient:

    Search medicine

    ↓

    Buy medicine

    ↓

    Experience side effects

    ↓

    Contact company

    User Journey

    Experience while using a specific product.

    Example:

    Safety reviewer using AI platform:

    Login

    ↓

    Review case

    ↓

    Approve report

    Difference:

    Customer Journey User Journey

    Entire relationship Specific product usage

    Marketing/service focus Product usability focus

    Customer experience User interaction

    9. Personas in BA

    A persona is:

    A fictional representation of a real user group based on research.

    Example:

    Persona 1

    Safety Reviewer

    Goals:

    • Process cases quickly

    • Reduce errors

    Challenges:

    • High workload

    • Manual documentation

    Persona 2

    Medical Reviewer

    Goals:

    • Make accurate clinical decisions

    Challenges:

    • Missing information

    10. Touchpoints

    Touchpoint:

    Any interaction between user and product/service.

    AI Pharmacovigilance Example:

    Touchpoints:

    1. Login screen

    2. Dashboard

    3. Case upload

    4. AI summary screen

    5. Approval page

    6. Notification

    11. Pain Point Analysis

    BA identifies:

    Current Problem

    ↓

    Root Cause

    ↓

    Improvement Opportunity

    Example:

    Pain:

    Reviewers spend 30 minutes per case.

    Root Cause:

    Manual extraction.

    Opportunity:

    AI extraction.

    12. User Journey + AI Product Example

    Product:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

    Persona:

    Safety Reviewer

    Journey:

    Stage 1: Receive Case

    Action:

    Open new case.

    Pain:

    High workload.

    Opportunity:

    AI prioritization.

    Stage 2: Analyze Document

    Action:

    Read reports.

    Pain:

    Large documents.

    Opportunity:

    AI extraction.

    Stage 3: Review AI Output

    Action:

    Validate suggestions.

    Pain:

    Trust issues.

    Opportunity:

    Explainable AI.

    Stage 4: Approval

    Action:

    Approve case.

    Pain:

    Compliance concerns.

    Opportunity:

    Audit trail.

    13. Workflow vs User Journey Relationship

    They work together.

    Example:

    Workflow:

    AI extracts data

    ↓

    Reviewer validates

    User Journey:

    Reviewer feels relieved because manual entry is reduced.

    14. BA Interview Questions

    Q1: Difference between workflow and user journey?

    Answer:

    \"Workflow focuses on the sequence of activities required to complete a business task, while user journey focuses on the user\'s experience, emotions, and interactions throughout that process.\"

    Q2: Why create user journey maps?

    Answer:

    \"User journey maps help identify user needs, pain points, opportunities, and improve product experiences.\"

    Q3: What is a persona?

    Answer:

    \"A persona is a research-based representation of a target user group used to guide product and requirement decisions.\"

    Practical Assignment

    Create a User Journey Map for:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

    Persona:

    Safety Reviewer

    Create:

    1. User Goal

    2. Journey Stages

    3. User Actions

    4. Thoughts

    5. Pain Points

    6. Opportunities

    Minimum:

    5 journey stages.

    Lesson Summary

    You learned:

    ✅ Process vs Workflow vs User Flow vs User Journey\ ✅ Workflow design\ ✅ User Journey Mapping\ ✅ Customer Journey vs User Journey\ ✅ Personas\ ✅ Touchpoints\ ✅ Pain points\ ✅ Healthcare AI journey example

    Lesson 20: Wireframing & Prototyping for Business Analysts

    Translating Requirements into Product Experiences

    A BA does not only describe what the system should do. A great BA helps stakeholders visualize how the solution will work.

    Many requirement problems happen because people interpret written requirements differently.

    Example:

    Requirement:

    \"Create an AI dashboard for safety reviewers.\"

    Different people imagine different things:

    Business thinks:

    • KPI dashboard

    Developer thinks:

    • Data tables

    User thinks:

    • Easy case review screen

    A wireframe removes ambiguity.

    Learning Objectives

    By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

    • Why BAs need wireframing skills

    • Difference between wireframe and prototype

    • Low, medium, and high fidelity designs

    • Screen design principles

    • User flow to wireframe conversion

    • Prototype validation

    • Healthcare AI example

    1. What is Wireframing?

    Definition:

    A wireframe is a visual blueprint of a product interface that shows screen structure, layout, navigation, and functionality.

    Simple:

    A wireframe is like an architectural drawing of a house before construction.

    Example:

    Before building:

    \"AI Case Review Screen\"

    BA creates:

    --------------------------------

    Case ID: AE-10234

    Patient Information

    -------------------------------

    AI Summary

    [Generated Summary]

    -------------------------------

    Risk Level: HIGH

    -------------------------------

    [Approve] [Reject]

    --------------------------------

    2. Why BAs Need Wireframing

    A BA uses wireframes to:

    ✅ Clarify requirements\ ✅ Validate user expectations\ ✅ Identify missing functionality\ ✅ Improve stakeholder communication\ ✅ Reduce development rework

    Example:

    Requirement:

    \"User should review AI output.\"

    Questions appear:

    • Where is AI output shown?

    • Can user edit it?

    • Can user approve?

    • Can user compare original vs AI?

    Wireframe answers these.

    3. Wireframe vs Mockup vs Prototype

    Very common interview topic.

    Wireframe

    Focus:

    Structure and layout.

    Usually:

    • Black and white

    • Simple blocks

    • No detailed styling

    Example:

    Header

    Menu

    Content Area

    Button

    Mockup

    Focus:

    Visual appearance.

    Includes:

    • Colors

    • Fonts

    • Images

    • Branding

    Prototype

    Focus:

    Interaction.

    Shows:

    • Clicks

    • Navigation

    • User flow

    Example:

    Click:

    \"Generate Summary\"

    ↓

    Moves to summary screen.

    Comparison:

                    **Wireframe**     **Mockup**         **Prototype**

    Structure ✅ ✅ ✅

    Design ❌ ✅ ✅

    Interaction ❌ ❌/Limited ✅

    4. Fidelity Levels

    Fidelity means:

    \"How close is the design to the final product?\"

    Level 1: Low Fidelity Wireframe

    Simple sketch.

    Purpose:

    Early discussion.

    Example:

    +----------------+

    | Dashboard |

    +----------------+

    | Cases |

    | Button |

    Used during:

    Requirement discovery.

    Level 2: Medium Fidelity

    More detail.

    Includes:

    • Real fields

    • Navigation

    • Components

    Example:

    Case Dashboard

    Search Case [____]

    Filters:

    Status

    Priority

    Date

    Case List

    Level 3: High Fidelity

    Almost final design.

    Includes:

    • Colors

    • Branding

    • Real interactions

    Used for:

    User testing.

    5. Wireframe Creation Process

    A BA follows:

    Understand Requirement

    ↓

    Identify User Goal

    ↓

    Create User Flow

    ↓

    Design Screens

    ↓

    Review With Stakeholders

    ↓

    Improve

    6. From User Story to Wireframe

    Example User Story:

    As a safety reviewer, I want AI-generated summaries so that I can review cases faster.

    Convert into screens:

    Screen 1:

    Login

    ↓

    Screen 2:

    Case Dashboard

    ↓

    Screen 3:

    Case Details

    ↓

    Screen 4:

    AI Summary Review

    ↓

    Screen 5:

    Approval

    7. AI Pharmacovigilance Wireframe Example

    Screen: Case Review Dashboard

    ------------------------------------------------

    AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

    Search Case: __________

    Filters:

    [High Risk] [Pending] [Approved]

    Case List

    ------------------------------------------------

    Case ID Drug Risk Status

    AE-001 Drug A High Review

    AE-002 Drug B Low Approved

    ------------------------------------------------

    Screen: AI Summary Review

    ------------------------------------------------

    Case ID: AE-001

    Patient Details

    Age:

    Gender:

    History:

    AI Generated Summary

    --------------------------------

    Patient experienced headache

    after medication administration.

    Confidence Score:

    92%

    [Edit] [Approve]

    ------------------------------------------------

    8. Important BA Wireframe Components

    A BA should understand:

    Navigation

    How users move.

    Example:

    Dashboard → Case → Review → Approval

    Information Architecture

    How information is organized.

    Example:

    Case information:

    • Patient details

    • Drug information

    • Event details

    User Actions

    What users can do.

    Example:

    • Upload

    • Edit

    • Approve

    • Reject

    Validation Messages

    Example:

    \"Required field missing.\"

    Error Handling

    Example:

    \"AI extraction failed. Manual entry required.\"

    9. Prototype

    Definition:

    A prototype is an interactive representation of a product used to test ideas and collect feedback before development.

    Example:

    Stakeholder clicks:

    Upload Document

    ↓

    AI Processing

    ↓

    Summary Generated

    ↓

    Approve

    No actual backend exists.

    It demonstrates experience.

    10. Why Prototype Before Development?

    Without prototype:

    Team builds:

    ❌ Wrong workflow\ ❌ Missing screens\ ❌ Poor usability

    With prototype:

    Users say:

    \"This button should be here.\"

    before coding starts.

    11. Prototype Validation Process

    BA conducts:

    Step 1

    Show prototype.

    Step 2

    Ask user to complete tasks.

    Example:

    \"Review this case.\"

    Step 3

    Observe:

    • Confusion

    • Missing information

    • User feedback

    Step 4

    Improve design.

    12. Wireframe Tools Used by BAs

    Common tools:

    • Figma

    • Balsamiq

    • Axure RP

    • Adobe XD

    13. BA Role vs UX Designer Role

    Important distinction.

    A BA is not replacing a UX designer.

    BA Focus:

    • Business needs

    • User requirements

    • Workflow

    • Functional behavior

    UX Designer Focus:

    • Visual design

    • Interaction patterns

    • Usability

    • Design system

    They collaborate.

    14. Healthcare AI Example

    Requirement:

    \"Reviewer should validate AI recommendations.\"

    BA identifies:

    Need:

    A review screen.

    Wireframe includes:

    Left Panel:

    Original document

    Center:

    AI extracted information

    Right Panel:

    AI recommendation

    Bottom:

    Approve / Modify / Reject

    Why?

    Because reviewer needs comparison and control.

    15. Interview Questions

    Q1: Why do BAs create wireframes?

    Answer:

    \"Wireframes help visualize requirements, validate user expectations, identify missing functionality, and reduce misunderstanding between business and development teams.\"

    Q2: Difference between wireframe and prototype?

    Answer:

    \"A wireframe shows structure and layout, while a prototype demonstrates interaction and user flow.\"

    Q3: What is fidelity?

    Answer:

    \"Fidelity represents the level of detail and realism in a design, ranging from low-fidelity sketches to high-fidelity interactive designs.\"

    Practical Assignment

    Create a wireframe specification for:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

    Design these screens:

    1. Login Screen

    Include:

    • User authentication

    • Role selection

    2. Dashboard

    Include:

    • Case count

    • Pending reviews

    • Risk alerts

    3. Case Review Screen

    Include:

    • Patient details

    • AI extraction

    • AI summary

    • Approval actions

    4. Approval Screen

    Include:

    • Reviewer comments

    • Approve/reject actions

    You learned:

    ✅ Wireframing concepts\ ✅ Prototype concepts\ ✅ Low/Mid/High fidelity\ ✅ Requirement to screen conversion\ ✅ User flow design\ ✅ Prototype validation\ ✅ BA vs UX designer role\ ✅ Healthcare AI example

    Lesson 21: Solution Design & Requirements Validation

    From Requirements to an Approved Solution

    A BA\'s responsibility does not end after writing requirements. A BA ensures that the proposed solution actually solves the business problem.

    Many projects fail because:

    • Requirements were misunderstood

    • Stakeholders were not aligned

    • Solution does not solve the real problem

    • Business rules were missed

    • Users reject the final product

    Solution validation prevents this.

    Learning Objectives

    By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

    • What is solution design

    • BA role in solution design

    • Functional solution design

    • Non-functional considerations

    • Business rules validation

    • Requirement walkthrough

    • Design review

    • Stakeholder approval

    • Prototype feedback loop

    • Healthcare AI solution example

    1. What is Solution Design?

    Definition:

    Solution design is the process of defining how a proposed solution will meet business needs, user requirements, and technical constraints.

    Simple:

    Requirement:

    \"Reduce pharmacovigilance processing time.\"

    Solution design:

    \"Create an AI-assisted workflow that extracts case data, generates summaries, and supports human validation.\"

    2. Requirement vs Solution Design

    Very important difference.

    Requirement

    Describes:

    What the business needs

    Example:

    \"Reviewer should identify serious adverse events.\"

    Solution Design

    Describes:

    How the solution will enable it

    Example:

    \"AI model analyzes event description and highlights potential seriousness indicators.\"

    Comparison:

    Requirement Solution

    What How

    Business need Implementation approach

    User expectation System behavior

    3. BA Role in Solution Design

    A BA works between:

    Business

    ↓

    Business Analyst

    ↓

    Product / UX / Development Team

    ↓

    Solution

    BA responsibilities:

    ✅ Understand problem\ ✅ Define solution options\ ✅ Validate requirements\ ✅ Identify gaps\ ✅ Ensure user needs are addressed\ ✅ Support design decisions

    4. Solution Design Components

    A BA considers:

    1. Functional Design

    What the system should do.

    Example:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Platform:

    Functional requirements:

    • Upload documents

    • Extract information

    • Generate summary

    • Assign risk score

    • Allow approval

    2. Non-Functional Design

    How the system should perform.

    Examples:

    Performance

    \"Dashboard loads within 3 seconds.\"

    Security

    \"Only authorized reviewers can access cases.\"

    Reliability

    \"System availability should be 99.9%.\"

    Compliance

    \"Maintain audit trail.\"

    5. Business Rules Validation

    Business rules define constraints.

    Example:

    Rule:

    Serious adverse events must be submitted within 15 days.

    BA converts this into system behavior:

    IF:

    Case seriousness = Serious

    AND

    Approval completed

    THEN:

    Submission deadline = 15 days

    Business Rule Format

    A BA often documents:

    Field Example

    Rule ID BR-001

    Rule Name Serious Case Submission

    Condition Serious event identified

    Action Submit within 15 days

    Exception Missing information

    6. Requirement Validation

    Before development starts, BA validates:

    Is requirement complete?

    Example:

    \"System should generate reports.\"

    Questions:

    • Which reports?

    • Who generates?

    • Format?

    • Frequency?

    • Data source?

    Is requirement clear?

    Bad:

    \"System should be fast.\"

    Good:

    \"System response time should be below 3 seconds.\"

    Is requirement testable?

    Bad:

    \"Easy to use.\"

    Good:

    \"User can complete case review within 5 steps.\"

    7. Requirement Walkthrough

    Definition:

    A requirement walkthrough is a structured review session where stakeholders validate requirements before implementation.

    Participants:

    • Business stakeholders

    • Product Owner

    • BA

    • Developers

    • QA

    • Compliance team

    Agenda:

    1. Review requirement

    2. Explain workflow

    3. Confirm assumptions

    4. Identify missing details

    5. Approve

    Example Walkthrough

    Requirement:

    \"AI should generate case summaries.\"

    Questions:

    Developer:

    \"What input format?\"

    QA:

    \"How do we test accuracy?\"

    Compliance:

    \"Can AI output be used directly?\"

    User:

    \"Can I edit the summary?\"

    BA captures answers.

    8. Solution Review Process

    A BA performs:

    Step 1: Review Current Problem

    Example:

    Manual case processing takes 5 days.

    Step 2: Review Proposed Solution

    AI reduces manual effort.

    Step 3: Validate Against Requirements

    Check:

    Does solution solve original problem?

    Step 4: Identify Gaps

    Example:

    Missing:

    Audit trail.

    Step 5: Get Approval

    Stakeholders accept solution.

    9. Prototype Feedback Loop

    Prototype is not final.

    It is improved through feedback.

    Process:

    Create Prototype

    ↓

    User Testing

    ↓

    Collect Feedback

    ↓

    Modify Design

    ↓

    Validate Again

    Example:

    First prototype:

    AI summary below document.

    User feedback:

    \"I need AI confidence score.\"

    Update:

    Add confidence indicator.

    10. Healthcare AI Solution Example

    Problem:

    Safety reviewers spend too much time reading reports.

    Requirement:

    \"System should help reviewers process cases faster.\"

    Solution Design:

    User Interface:

    Case dashboard

    ↓

    AI summary screen

    ↓

    Review screen

    Functional Design:

    System should:

    1. Upload documents

    2. Extract patient details

    3. Identify drug-event relationship

    4. Generate summary

    5. Allow reviewer approval

    Business Rules:

    • Reviewer approval mandatory

    • AI cannot submit automatically

    • Serious cases require escalation

    Non-functional:

    • Secure access

    • Audit logs

    • Response under 5 seconds

    11. Requirement Dependency Analysis

    Before development, BA identifies dependencies.

    Example:

    Feature:

    AI Risk Prediction

    Depends on:

    • Historical case data

    • ML model availability

    • Data quality

    Dependency types:

    Internal

    Within project.

    Example:

    Dashboard depends on database.

    External

    Outside project.

    Example:

    Regulatory API integration.

    12. Decision Log

    BA maintains decisions.

    Example:

    Decision Reason

    Human approval required Compliance

    AI cannot auto-submit Regulatory risk

    Use existing login system Reduce development effort

    13. Traceability During Solution Design

    Requirement:

    ↓

    Design

    ↓

    Development

    ↓

    Testing

    ↓

    Release

    Example:

    BR-001

    Reduce processing time

    ↓

    FR-005

    AI Summary Generation

    ↓

    US-101

    Reviewer generates summary

    ↓

    TC-101

    Verify summary generation

    14. Interview Questions

    Q1: What is solution design?

    Answer:

    \"Solution design defines how a proposed solution will satisfy business requirements while considering functionality, usability, technical constraints, and business rules.\"

    Q2: How does a BA validate requirements?

    Answer:

    \"A BA validates requirements through walkthroughs, stakeholder reviews, prototypes, acceptance criteria, and ensuring requirements are complete, consistent, and testable.\"

    Q3: Difference between functional and non-functional requirements?

    Answer:

    \"Functional requirements describe what the system does, while non-functional requirements describe quality attributes such as performance, security, and reliability.\"

    Practical Assignment

    For the AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot:

    Prepare a mini Solution Design Document.

    Include:

    1. Business Problem

    Example:\ Manual case processing delays.

    2. Proposed Solution

    AI-assisted case processing.

    3. Functional Requirements

    Minimum 5.

    4. Non-Functional Requirements

    Minimum 5.

    5. Business Rules

    Minimum 3.

    6. Dependencies

    Minimum 3.

    Lesson Summary

    You learned:

    ✅ Solution design concepts\ ✅ BA role in solution design\ ✅ Functional requirements\ ✅ Non-functional requirements\ ✅ Business rules\ ✅ Requirement validation\ ✅ Walkthroughs\ ✅ Prototype feedback\ ✅ Dependencies\ ✅ Decision logs

    PHASE 4 -- Advanced BA Execution & Industry Delivery

    Lesson 22: BA Role in Agile Delivery

    How a Business Analyst Works Inside a Real Scrum Team

    A BA\'s work is not only before development. In Agile, a BA continuously collaborates with the team throughout the product lifecycle.

    Many beginners think:

    \"BA writes requirements and gives them to developers.\"

    That is a traditional waterfall mindset.

    In Agile:

    \"BA helps the team understand the problem, refine requirements, validate solutions, and deliver business value continuously.\"

    Learning Objectives

    By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

    • BA role in Agile

    • BA vs Product Owner

    • BA activities before, during, and after Sprint

    • BA involvement in Scrum events

    • Requirement clarification

    • Working with Developers

    • Working with QA

    • Supporting UAT

    • Real healthcare AI example

    1. Where Does a BA Fit in Agile?

    A typical Agile team:

    Product Owner

    |

    |

    --------------------------------

    | |

    Business Analyst Scrum Master

    |

    |

    Developers ---- QA Engineers ---- UX Designers

    The BA acts as a bridge:

    Business Problem

    ↓

    Business Analyst

    ↓

    Agile Team

    ↓

    Working Product

    2. BA Responsibilities in Agile

    A BA helps with:

    Requirement Discovery

    Understanding:

    • Business goals

    • User needs

    • Problems

    Backlog Management

    Helping create:

    • User Stories

    • Acceptance Criteria

    • Priorities

    Requirement Clarification

    During development:

    • Answer questions

    • Remove ambiguity

    • Explain business rules

    Validation

    Check:

    \"Did we build the right thing?\"

    3. BA vs Product Owner

    Very common interview question.

    Both roles work closely but are different.

    Product Owner Focus

    Question:

    \"What should we build and why?\"

    Responsibilities:

    • Product vision

    • Roadmap

    • Prioritization

    • Business value

    • Backlog ownership

    Business Analyst Focus

    Question:

    \"How should the requirement be understood and implemented?\"

    Responsibilities:

    • Requirement analysis

    • Process modeling

    • User stories

    • Acceptance criteria

    • Detailed clarification

    Comparison

    Product Owner Business Analyst

    Owns product direction Analyzes requirements

    Decides priority Defines details

    Customer value Requirement clarity

    Roadmap Execution support

    Example:

    Requirement:

    \"Create AI risk prediction feature.\"

    Product Owner decides:

    \"We need this feature because it reduces review time.\"

    BA defines:

    • User story

    • Workflow

    • Business rules

    • Acceptance criteria

    • Data requirements

    4. BA Activities Before Sprint

    Before development begins:

    1. Requirement Gathering

    Example:

    Interview safety reviewers.

    Questions:

    • Current problems?

    • Required workflow?

    • Pain points?

    2. Process Analysis

    BA creates:

    • AS-IS process

    • TO-BE process

    3. User Stories

    Example:

    As a safety reviewer,

    I want AI to identify potential risks,

    so that I can prioritize cases.

    4. Acceptance Criteria

    Example:

    Given:

    A case is uploaded

    When:

    AI analyzes the document

    Then:

    Risk score should be displayed.

    5. BA During Backlog Refinement

    Backlog refinement is where BA is heavily involved.

    Activities:

    Explain User Stories

    Developer asks:

    \"What exactly should happen?\"

    BA explains.

    Identify Missing Information

    Example:

    Story:

    \"Generate AI summary.\"

    Questions:

    • Summary format?

    • Length?

    • Editable?

    • Approval required?

    Split Large Stories

    Epic:

    \"AI Case Management\"

    Break into:

    1. Upload documents

    2. Extract information

    3. Generate summary

    4. Review output

    6. BA During Sprint Planning

    Sprint Planning:

    Team decides:

    \"What can we complete this sprint?\"

    BA supports by:

    Clarifying Scope

    Example:

    \"This story includes only AI extraction, not prediction.\"

    Explaining Acceptance Criteria

    Highlighting Dependencies

    Example:

    \"Dashboard depends on case database.\"

    7. BA During Development Sprint

    Many people think BA is inactive here.

    Wrong.

    BA continuously supports.

    Activities:

    Requirement Questions

    Developer:

    \"What happens if AI confidence is low?\"

    BA:

    \"Send case for manual review.\"

    Business Rule Clarification

    Developer:

    \"Can rejected cases be edited?\"

    BA:

    \"Yes, before final submission.\"

    Requirement Updates

    If changes occur:

    Update:

    • User story

    • Acceptance criteria

    • Documentation

    8. BA Working With Developers

    Good BA behavior:

    Instead of:

    \"Build exactly this.\"

    Say:

    \"The business problem is this. Here is the expected behavior.\"

    Example:

    Developer asks:

    \"How should risk score display?\"

    BA explains:

    Business need:

    Reviewer should identify high-risk cases quickly.

    Possible solution:

    • Color indicator

    • Score percentage

    • Priority label

    9. BA Working With QA Team

    BA helps QA understand:

    \"What should be tested?\"

    Example User Story:

    AI generates summary.

    Acceptance Criteria:

    Given:

    Case document uploaded

    When:

    AI processing completes

    Then:

    Summary appears with confidence score.

    QA creates:

    Test:

    Upload document → Verify summary.

    10. BA During Sprint Review

    Sprint Review:

    Team demonstrates completed work.

    BA checks:

    • Does feature solve user problem?

    • Does it match requirements?

    • Is stakeholder feedback captured?

    Example:

    Demo:

    AI Summary Screen.

    BA asks:

    \"Can reviewer edit AI output?\"

    If not:

    New requirement created.

    11. BA During Retrospective

    Retrospective focuses on:

    \"How can we improve?\"

    BA contributes:

    • Requirement quality issues

    • Communication gaps

    • Process improvements

    Example:

    Problem:

    Developers misunderstood business rules.

    Improvement:

    Add better acceptance criteria.

    12. BA Supporting UAT

    User Acceptance Testing:

    Business users verify solution.

    BA responsibilities:

    • Prepare UAT scenarios

    • Coordinate users

    • Capture defects

    • Confirm acceptance

    Example:

    Scenario:

    \"Reviewer approves high-risk case.\"

    Expected:

    Audit record created.

    13. Real Healthcare AI Example

    Project:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

    Before Sprint

    BA:

    Creates:

    • User stories

    • Acceptance criteria

    • Workflow diagrams

    During Sprint

    Developer:

    Builds AI extraction.

    BA:

    Clarifies:

    • Required fields

    • Validation rules

    • Exception handling

    QA Testing

    BA validates:

    Does extraction support business needs?

    Sprint Review

    Safety reviewers test.

    Feedback:

    \"Need confidence score.\"

    BA creates improvement story.

    14. BA Daily Activities Example

    A typical Agile BA day:

    Time Activity

    Morning Scrum meeting

    10 AM Developer clarification

    11 AM Requirement analysis

    Afternoon Stakeholder discussion

    Evening Update stories/documentation

    15. Interview Questions

    Q1: What is the role of BA in Agile?

    Answer:

    \"A BA helps the Agile team understand business needs, refine requirements, create user stories, clarify requirements during development, and ensure delivered features provide business value.\"

    Q2: Does BA replace Product Owner?

    Answer:

    \"No. The Product Owner owns product vision and priorities, while the BA focuses on requirement analysis and helping the team understand implementation details.\"

    Q3: How does BA support developers?

    Answer:

    \"BA provides requirement clarification, business rules, workflows, acceptance criteria, and resolves ambiguity during development.\"

    Practical Assignment

    For AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot:

    Create:

    User Story

    Feature:

    AI Case Summary Generation

    1. User Story

    2. Acceptance Criteria

    3. Business Rules

    4. Edge Cases

    5. Dependencies

    ✅ BA role in Agile\ ✅ BA vs Product Owner\ ✅ Sprint activities\ ✅ Backlog refinement\ ✅ Sprint planning support\ ✅ Developer collaboration\ ✅ QA collaboration\ ✅ UAT support\ ✅ Healthcare Agile example

    Lesson 23: Advanced User Stories & Acceptance Criteria Masterclass

    Writing Requirements Like an Industry Business Analyst

    A good BA does not write requirements as features. A good BA writes requirements as user problems and expected outcomes.

    Many beginners write:

    ❌ \"Create AI dashboard.\"\ ❌ \"Add risk prediction button.\"\ ❌ \"Build report page.\"

    These are system instructions.

    An Agile BA writes:

    ✅ Who needs it?\ ✅ What do they want?\ ✅ Why do they need it?\ ✅ How do we know it works?

    Learning Objectives

    By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

    • Epic → Feature → User Story hierarchy

    • User Story structure

    • INVEST principle

    • Writing professional user stories

    • Acceptance Criteria

    • Given-When-Then format

    • Business rules

    • Negative scenarios

    • Edge cases

    • AI product examples

    1. Requirement Hierarchy in Agile

    Large requirements are broken into smaller pieces.

    Structure:

    Product Vision

    ↓

    Epic

    ↓

    Feature

    ↓

    User Story

    ↓

    Task

    Example: AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

    Product Vision

    \"Reduce adverse event processing time using AI.\"

    Epic

    AI-Assisted Case Processing

    Features

    1. AI Document Extraction

    2. AI Summary Generation

    3. Risk Prediction

    4. Automated Classification

    User Stories

    Example:

    \"As a safety reviewer, I want AI to extract patient information so that I can reduce manual data entry.\"

    2. What is a User Story?

    Definition:

    A user story is a short description of a user\'s need written from the user\'s perspective.

    Standard format:

    As a \<user>

    I want \<goal>

    So that \<benefit>

    Example:

    As a pharmacovigilance reviewer,

    I want AI to generate case summaries,

    So that I can review adverse events faster.

    3. Three Important Parts

    1. User (Who?)

    Who needs this?

    Examples:

    • Safety reviewer

    • Doctor

    • Patient

    • Administrator

    2. Goal (What?)

    What capability is needed?

    Example:

    Generate AI summary.

    3. Benefit (Why?)

    Why does it matter?

    Example:

    Reduce review time.

    4. Bad vs Good User Stories

    Bad:

    \"Create AI module.\"

    Problems:

    ❌ No user\ ❌ No purpose\ ❌ No value

    Good:

    \"As a safety reviewer, I want AI to identify potential safety risks so that I can prioritize urgent cases.\"

    5. INVEST Principle

    A professional user story should follow INVEST.

    Created by:

    Bill Wake

    I -- Independent

    Story should stand alone.

    Bad:

    \"Create approval button.\"

    Depends on:

    \"Create review screen.\"

    Better:

    \"Reviewer can approve validated cases.\"

    N -- Negotiable

    Story is not a fixed contract.

    The team can discuss implementation.

    V -- Valuable

    Must provide user/business value.

    Bad:

    \"Add database field.\"

    No user value.

    E -- Estimable

    Team should understand enough to estimate effort.

    S -- Small

    Should fit inside a sprint.

    T -- Testable

    Should have clear acceptance criteria.

    6. User Story Example (Healthcare AI)

    Story:

    As a safety reviewer,

    I want the system to highlight serious adverse events,

    So that I can prioritize high-risk cases.

    Acceptance Criteria:

    Given a case is uploaded,

    When AI analyzes the event,

    Then serious indicators should be displayed.

    Given a high-risk case,

    When reviewer opens dashboard,

    Then the case should appear as high priority.

    7. Acceptance Criteria

    Definition:

    Acceptance criteria define the conditions that must be satisfied for a user story to be considered complete.

    Simple:

    User Story:

    \"What user needs\"

    Acceptance Criteria:

    \"How we verify it works\"

    Example:

    Story:

    AI generates summary.

    Acceptance Criteria:

    • Summary generated after document upload

    • User can edit summary

    • System stores final version

    • Audit log is created

    8. Given-When-Then Format

    Most common Agile format.

    Also called:

    BDD (Behavior Driven Development)

    Structure:

    Given

    (initial condition)

    When

    (action)

    Then

    (expected result)

    Example:

    Feature:

    AI Risk Classification

    Scenario:

    High-risk case identification

    Given a safety case contains serious adverse event indicators

    When AI analyzes the case

    Then the system should mark the case as high risk

    9. Multiple Acceptance Criteria Example

    User Story:

    \"As a reviewer, I want AI-generated summaries so that I can reduce review time.\"

    AC1: Generate Summary

    Given a document is uploaded

    When AI processing completes

    Then a summary should be displayed

    AC2: Confidence Score

    Given AI generates a summary

    When confidence is calculated

    Then confidence percentage should be visible

    AC3: Low Confidence

    Given AI confidence is below threshold

    When summary is generated

    Then case should be flagged for manual review

    10. Business Rules Inside User Stories

    Business rules define restrictions.

    Example:

    Rule:

    \"Only medical reviewers can approve serious cases.\"

    User Story:

    \"As a medical reviewer, I want to approve serious cases so that compliance requirements are followed.\"

    Acceptance Criteria:

    Given a serious case

    When a safety reviewer attempts approval

    Then approval option should not be available

    11. Negative Scenarios

    Professional BAs always consider failures.

    Example:

    Normal:

    Upload document → AI processes → Summary generated

    Negative:

    Upload corrupted file.

    Expected:

    System shows:

    \"Unable to process document. Upload valid file.\"

    12. Edge Cases

    Edge cases are unusual situations.

    Example:

    AI Summary Feature:

    Normal:

    100-page document.

    Edge:

    1000-page document.

    Questions:

    • Should processing continue?

    • Should user receive notification?

    • Should system split document?

    13. Definition of Ready (DoR)

    Before development starts, story should be ready.

    Checklist:

    ✅ Clear user value\ ✅ Acceptance criteria defined\ ✅ Dependencies identified\ ✅ Estimated\ ✅ Stakeholders agree

    14. Definition of Done (DoD)

    When is story complete?

    Example:

    AI Summary Story:

    ✅ Code completed\ ✅ Tested\ ✅ Security checked\ ✅ User acceptance completed\ ✅ Documentation updated

    15. Epic Breakdown Example

    Epic:

    AI Case Processing

    Feature:

    AI Document Extraction

    Stories:

    Story 1

    Upload document

    Story 2

    Extract patient details

    Story 3

    Extract drug information

    Story 4

    Validate extracted data

    Story 5

    Store extracted information

    16. Complete BA Example

    Feature:

    AI Risk Prediction

    User Story:

    As a safety reviewer,

    I want AI to predict case risk level,

    So that I can prioritize critical cases.

    Acceptance Criteria:

    Scenario 1:

    Given a case contains complete information

    When AI analyzes the case

    Then risk score should be generated

    Scenario 2:

    Given risk score is high

    When reviewer opens dashboard

    Then case should be highlighted as priority

    Scenario 3:

    Given AI confidence is low

    When prediction is generated

    Then reviewer should receive a warning

    17. Interview Questions

    Q1: What is a user story?

    Answer:

    \"A user story is a short description of a user\'s requirement written from the user\'s perspective, explaining who needs something, what they need, and why.\"

    Q2: What is INVEST?

    Answer:

    \"INVEST defines qualities of a good user story: Independent, Negotiable, Valuable, Estimable, Small, and Testable.\"

    Q3: Difference between requirement and acceptance criteria?

    Answer:

    \"Requirement describes the capability needed, while acceptance criteria define conditions to verify that the capability works correctly.\"

    Practical Assignment

    Create user stories for:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

    Create 5 stories:

    1. Document Upload

    2. AI Extraction

    3. AI Summary

    4. Risk Prediction

    5. Regulatory Submission

    For each include:

    • User Story

    • Acceptance Criteria (Given-When-Then)

    • Business Rules

    • Edge Cases

    Lesson Summary

    You learned:

    ✅ Epic → Feature → Story hierarchy\ ✅ User Story writing\ ✅ INVEST principle\ ✅ Acceptance Criteria\ ✅ Given-When-Then\ ✅ Business rules\ ✅ Negative scenarios\ ✅ Edge cases\ ✅ Definition of Ready\ ✅ Definition of Done

    Lesson 24: Advanced Requirements Documentation

    BRD, FRD, SRS, RTM & Professional BA Documentation

    A Business Analyst is judged not only by understanding requirements but also by the quality of documentation created to communicate those requirements.

    In real projects, a BA creates different documents for different audiences.

    Example:

    Business executives need:

    \"Why are we building this?\"

    Developers need:

    \"What exactly should the system do?\"

    QA needs:

    \"How do we verify it works?\"

    Each requires different documentation.

    Learning Objectives

    By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

    • BA documentation ecosystem

    • BRD vs FRD vs SRS

    • Business Requirements Document

    • Functional Requirements Document

    • Software Requirements Specification

    • Requirement Traceability Matrix

    • Business Rules Document

    • When to use each document

    • Healthcare AI examples

    1. BA Documentation Landscape

    A typical BA documentation flow:

    Business Need

    ↓

    BRD

    (Business Requirements)

    ↓

    FRD

    (Functional Requirements)

    ↓

    SRS

    (System Requirements)

    ↓

    User Stories

    ↓

    Development

    ↓

    Testing

    2. BRD (Business Requirements Document)

    What is BRD?

    Definition:

    BRD describes the business problem, objectives, goals, scope, and high-level requirements from a business perspective.

    Audience:

    • Business leaders

    • Sponsors

    • Product owners

    • Stakeholders

    BRD Answers:

    Why are we building this?

    What business problem are we solving?

    What outcome do we expect?

    BRD Structure

    A professional BRD contains:

    1. Document Information

    Example:

    Project Name:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

    Version:

    1.0

    Author:

    Business Analyst

    2. Executive Summary

    Example:

    \"The organization wants to reduce adverse event processing time using AI-assisted automation.\"

    3. Business Problem

    Current situation:

    • Manual review process

    • Long processing time

    • High workload

    4. Business Objectives

    Example:

    Reduce case processing time by 50%.

    5. Scope

    In Scope:

    ✅ AI document extraction\ ✅ Case summarization\ ✅ Risk identification

    Out of Scope:

    ❌ Fully automated regulatory submission approval

    6. Stakeholders

    Example:

    Stakeholder Role

    Safety Team Primary users

    Medical Team Approval

    Compliance Regulatory review

    7. Business Requirements

    Example:

    BR-001:

    \"The system should reduce manual case review effort.\"

    8. Success Metrics

    Example:

    Before:

    5 days processing

    After:

    2 days processing

    3. FRD (Functional Requirements Document)

    What is FRD?

    Definition:

    FRD describes what the system must do to satisfy business requirements.

    Audience:

    • Developers

    • QA engineers

    • Architects

    BRD says:

    \"We need faster case processing.\"

    FRD says:

    \"System shall extract patient information automatically from uploaded documents.\"

    FRD Structure

    1. Functional Requirement ID

    Example:

    FR-001

    2. Feature Name

    AI Document Extraction

    3. Description

    System extracts:

    • Patient information

    • Drug details

    • Event information

    4. User Interaction

    Example:

    1. User uploads document

    2. System processes file

    3. Extracted information displayed

    5. Business Rules

    Example:

    Only authorized reviewers can edit extracted data.

    6. Acceptance Criteria

    Example:

    Given:

    Document uploaded

    When:

    AI processing completes

    Then:

    Extracted fields should display.

    4. SRS (Software Requirements Specification)

    What is SRS?

    Definition:

    SRS describes complete software requirements including functional and non-functional requirements.

    It is more technical than BRD/FRD.

    Audience:

    • Developers

    • Architects

    • QA

    • Technical teams

    SRS Includes:

    Functional Requirements

    What system does.

    Example:

    Login functionality.

    Non-Functional Requirements

    How system performs.

    Example:

    Response time \< 3 seconds.

    External Interfaces

    Example:

    Integration with:

    • Regulatory database

    • Drug database

    Security Requirements

    Example:

    Role-based access control.

    Data Requirements

    Example:

    Store:

    • Case ID

    • Patient information

    • Drug details

    5. BRD vs FRD vs SRS

    Very important interview question.

    BRD FRD SRS

    Business need System functionality Complete software specification

    Why What How system behaves

    Business users BA + Dev Technical team

    High level Detailed Detailed + technical

    Example:

    BRD:

    \"Reduce manual pharmacovigilance workload.\"

    FRD:

    \"System shall generate AI summaries.\"

    SRS:

    \"AI service shall process PDF documents using NLP pipeline and return summary within 10 seconds.\"

    6. Requirement Traceability Matrix (RTM)

    What is RTM?

    Definition:

    RTM ensures every requirement is tracked from business need to testing.

    Flow:

    Business Requirement

    ↓

    Functional Requirement

    ↓

    User Story

    ↓

    Development Task

    ↓

    Test Case

    RTM Example

    BR ID FR ID User Story Test Case

    BR-001 FR-001 Upload Document TC-001

    BR-002 FR-002 AI Summary TC-002

    Why RTM is Important?

    ✅ Prevent missing requirements\ ✅ Supports audits\ ✅ Helps impact analysis\ ✅ Ensures complete testing

    7. Business Rules Document

    Business rules define policies and decisions.

    Example:

    Rule:

    \"Serious adverse events must be reviewed by medical experts.\"

    Format:

    ID Rule Condition Action

    BRULE-001 Serious Case Review Risk = High Medical approval required

    8. Acceptance Criteria Document

    Sometimes maintained separately.

    Contains:

    • User scenarios

    • Expected behavior

    • Validation rules

    Example:

    Feature:

    AI Risk Score

    Criteria:

    Given case information exists

    When AI analyzes case

    Then risk score must be displayed

    9. Change Request Document

    Used when requirements change.

    Example:

    New request:

    \"Add multilingual AI support.\"

    Document contains:

    • Change description

    • Reason

    • Impact

    • Approval

    10. Complete Enterprise Example

    Project:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

    BRD

    Business Goal:

    Reduce case processing time.

    FRD

    Requirement:

    System should extract adverse event information.

    SRS

    Technical requirement:

    NLP engine processes uploaded documents and returns structured fields.

    User Story

    \"As a reviewer, I want AI extraction so that I can reduce manual entry.\"

    Acceptance Criteria

    Given:

    PDF uploaded

    When:

    Extraction completes

    Then:

    Patient and drug fields are populated.

    Test Case

    Upload sample report.

    Expected:

    Fields extracted correctly.

    11. BA Documentation Best Practices

    1. Use unique IDs

    Example:

    BR-001

    FR-001

    US-001

    2. Avoid ambiguous words

    Avoid:

    \"Fast\"

    Use:

    \"Response time below 3 seconds.\"

    3. Keep traceability

    Every requirement should connect to testing.

    4. Version control

    Maintain:

    • Version number

    • Date

    • Author

    • Change history

    5. Review before approval

    Never send incomplete documents.

    12. Interview Questions

    Q1: Difference between BRD and FRD?

    Answer:

    \"BRD defines business objectives and needs, while FRD defines the system functionalities required to achieve those objectives.\"

    Q2: What is RTM?

    Answer:

    \"RTM is a document that maps requirements throughout the lifecycle from business requirement to implementation and testing.\"

    Q3: Why are acceptance criteria important?

    Answer:

    \"Acceptance criteria remove ambiguity and provide clear conditions to determine whether a requirement is successfully implemented.\"

    Practical Assignment

    Create documentation structure for:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

    Prepare:

    1. BRD

    Include:

    • Business problem

    • Objectives

    • Scope

    • Stakeholders

    2. FRD

    Include:

    • 5 functional requirements

    3. RTM

    Map:

    BR → FR → User Story → Test Case

    Lesson Summary

    You learned:

    ✅ BRD\ ✅ FRD\ ✅ SRS\ ✅ RTM\ ✅ Business Rules Document\ ✅ Acceptance Criteria Document\ ✅ Change Request Document\ ✅ Documentation best practices\ ✅ Enterprise healthcare example

    Lesson 25: Stakeholder Management & Communication Masterclass

    Managing People, Expectations, Conflicts, and Decisions

    A BA spends less time writing documents and more time creating alignment between people.

    Many projects fail not because of technology problems, but because:

    • Stakeholders have different expectations

    • Requirements are misunderstood

    • Decisions are delayed

    • Teams are not aligned

    A senior BA is an alignment specialist.

    Learning Objectives

    By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

    • Who stakeholders are

    • Stakeholder identification techniques

    • Stakeholder analysis

    • Power-Interest Matrix

    • Stakeholder communication strategy

    • Managing conflicts

    • Requirement negotiation

    • Handling difficult stakeholders

    • Healthcare AI example

    1. What is Stakeholder Management?

    Definition:

    Stakeholder management is the process of identifying, analyzing, communicating with, and managing people who influence or are affected by a project.

    Simple:

    A stakeholder is anyone who:

    • Wants something from the project

    • Can impact the project

    • Is affected by the outcome

    2. Who is a Stakeholder?

    Stakeholders can be:

    Internal

    Inside organization:

    • Product Owner

    • Business team

    • Developers

    • QA

    • Management

    • Compliance

    External

    Outside organization:

    • Customers

    • Regulators

    • Vendors

    • Partners

    Healthcare AI Example

    Project:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

    Stakeholders:

    Stakeholder Interest

    Safety Reviewer Faster case review

    Medical Reviewer Clinical accuracy

    Compliance Team Regulatory compliance

    IT Team Security

    Management ROI

    Patients Safety improvement

    3. Stakeholder Identification

    A BA identifies stakeholders early.

    Methods:

    1. Organizational Analysis

    Look at:

    • Departments

    • Roles

    • Decision makers

    2. Process Analysis

    Ask:

    \"Who performs each step?\"

    Example:

    Case approval process:

    • Reviewer

    • Medical expert

    • Compliance officer

    3. User Research

    Understand:

    • Who uses product?

    • Who benefits?

    • Who may resist?

    4. Stakeholder Register

    A BA maintains stakeholder information.

    Example:

    Stakeholder Role Influence Interest

    Safety Head Sponsor High High

    Reviewer User Medium High

    IT Manager Technical High Medium

    5. Stakeholder Analysis

    Purpose:

    Understand:

    • Who matters most?

    • Who needs more communication?

    • Who can block progress?

    Important factors:

    Power

    Ability to influence decisions.

    Example:

    CEO has high power.

    Interest

    How much they care.

    Example:

    Daily user has high interest.

    6. Power-Interest Matrix

    One of the most important BA techniques.

    Four categories:

    HIGH POWER

    Manage Closely

    |

    |

    Keep Satisfied | Key Players

    |

    LOW INTEREST ---------------- HIGH INTEREST

    |

    Monitor | Keep Informed

    |

    LOW POWER

    Category 1: Manage Closely

    High Power + High Interest

    Examples:

    • Product Owner

    • Project Sponsor

    Strategy:

    Frequent communication.

    Category 2: Keep Satisfied

    High Power + Low Interest

    Example:

    Senior executives.

    Strategy:

    Provide important updates.

    Do not overload.

    Category 3: Keep Informed

    Low Power + High Interest

    Example:

    End users.

    Strategy:

    Regular communication.

    Category 4: Monitor

    Low Power + Low Interest

    Example:

    Indirect teams.

    Strategy:

    Minimal updates.

    7. Stakeholder Communication Plan

    A BA defines:

    Stakeholder Information Method Frequency

    Product Owner Progress Meeting Weekly

    Developers Requirements Workshop Daily

    Users Feedback Demo Sprint end

    8. Communication Styles

    Different stakeholders need different communication.

    Executives

    Need:

    • Business value

    • Cost

    • ROI

    • Risk

    Avoid:

    Technical details.

    Developers

    Need:

    • Logic

    • Rules

    • Edge cases

    • Data behavior

    Users

    Need:

    • Workflow

    • Benefits

    • Usability

    9. Requirement Negotiation

    Real projects have conflicts.

    Example:

    Business says:

    \"We want AI to automatically approve cases.\"

    Compliance says:

    \"Human approval is mandatory.\"

    BA role:

    Find balance.

    Negotiation steps:

    Step 1:

    Understand the conflict.

    Step 2:

    Identify underlying need.

    Step 3:

    Find alternatives.

    Step 4:

    Document decision.

    Example Resolution

    Original request:

    AI automatically approves cases.

    Alternative:

    AI recommends approval.

    Human reviewer confirms.

    Result:

    Business gets speed.

    Compliance gets control.

    10. Managing Difficult Stakeholders

    Common situations:

    Situation 1:

    Stakeholder keeps changing requirements.

    Problem:

    Scope creep.

    BA approach:

    Ask:

    \"Has the business objective changed?\"

    Evaluate:

    • Impact

    • Priority

    • Cost

    Situation 2:

    Stakeholder does not provide information.

    BA approach:

    • Schedule focused workshop

    • Provide questions beforehand

    • Explain impact of delay

    Situation 3:

    Two stakeholders disagree.

    BA approach:

    Focus on:

    • Business objective

    • Data

    • User impact

    Not personal opinions.

    11. Conflict Resolution Techniques

    Common techniques:

    Collaborating

    Best approach.

    Find win-win solution.

    Example:

    AI assistance + human approval.

    Compromising

    Both sides give something.

    Avoiding

    Use only when issue is minor.

    Accommodating

    One side accepts.

    Competing

    Used for urgent decisions.

    12. Stakeholder Workshop Facilitation

    A BA often conducts workshops.

    Workshop structure:

    Before Workshop

    Prepare:

    • Agenda

    • Participants

    • Questions

    • Documents

    During Workshop

    BA:

    • Controls discussion

    • Captures decisions

    • Clarifies ambiguity

    After Workshop

    Share:

    • Meeting notes

    • Decisions

    • Action items

    13. Decision Management

    BA maintains decision log.

    Example:

    Decision Owner Reason

    Human approval required Compliance Regulatory requirement

    AI confidence displayed Product Improve trust

    14. Healthcare AI Stakeholder Example

    Project:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot

    Stakeholder Conflict:

    Business Team:

    \"We need maximum automation.\"

    Compliance:

    \"AI cannot make final decisions.\"

    Users:

    \"We need faster workflow.\"

    BA solution:

    Design:

    AI recommends

    ↓

    Reviewer validates

    ↓

    System records audit trail

    Everyone\'s goal achieved.

    15. Interview Questions

    Q1: How do you identify stakeholders?

    Answer:

    \"I identify stakeholders by analyzing business processes, organizational structure, user roles, decision makers, and anyone impacted by the solution.\"

    Q2: How do you handle conflicting requirements?

    Answer:

    \"I understand the underlying business objectives, analyze impact, facilitate discussion, evaluate alternatives, and document the final decision.\"

    Q3: What is a stakeholder matrix?

    Answer:

    \"A stakeholder matrix categorizes stakeholders based on factors like power and interest to determine the appropriate engagement strategy.\"

    Practical Assignment

    For AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot:

    Create:

    1. Stakeholder Register

    Include 8 stakeholders.

    Columns:

    • Name/Role

    • Interest

    • Influence

    • Communication Method

    2. Power-Interest Matrix

    Classify:

    • High Power + High Interest

    • High Power + Low Interest

    • Low Power + High Interest

    • Low Power + Low Interest

    3. Conflict Scenario

    Create one conflict and explain BA resolution.
