    Lesson 36: Customer Journey vs User Flow Masterclass

    Understanding the Complete Experience From Business Perspective to Product Interaction

    A senior BA must understand three different views:

    1. Business Process View → How the organization works

    2. Customer Journey View → How the customer experiences the organization

    3. User Flow View → How a user completes a task inside the product

    Many BAs confuse these three concepts. Understanding the difference separates a requirement writer from a product-focused BA.

    Learning Objectives

    By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

    • Customer Journey Mapping

    • User Journey vs Customer Journey

    • User Flow Design

    • Customer lifecycle

    • Funnel analysis

    • Conversion points

    • Drop-off analysis

    • Healthcare digital product example

    • Converting journeys into requirements

    1. Customer Journey Mapping

    Definition

    Customer Journey Mapping represents the complete relationship between a customer and an organization across all stages of interaction.

    It focuses on:

    • Customer expectations

    • Business touchpoints

    • Satisfaction

    • Loyalty

    • Long-term relationship

    Simple:

    Customer Journey answers:

    \"What experience does the customer have with our company?\"

    Example:

    A pharmaceutical company adopting an AI Pharmacovigilance Platform.

    Customer Journey:

    Awareness

    ↓

    Evaluation

    ↓

    Purchase Decision

    ↓

    Implementation

    ↓

    Usage

    ↓

    Support

    ↓

    Renewal

    2. Customer Journey Components

    A professional customer journey map contains:

    1. Customer Persona

    2. Journey Stages

    3. Customer Goals

    4. Touchpoints

    5. Pain Points

    6. Emotions

    7. Business Opportunities

    3. Example: Healthcare AI Customer Journey

    Customer:

    Global Pharmaceutical Company

    Product:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

    Stage 1: Awareness

    Customer Goal:

    Find ways to improve safety operations.

    Touchpoints:

    • Industry conference

    • Website

    • Sales presentation

    Pain:

    Manual processes are expensive.

    Opportunity:

    Show AI efficiency benefits.

    Stage 2: Evaluation

    Customer Goal:

    Compare solutions.

    Touchpoints:

    • Product demo

    • Technical discussion

    • Security review

    Pain:

    Concern about:

    • Data privacy

    • AI accuracy

    Opportunity:

    Provide:

    • Security documentation

    • AI validation reports

    Stage 3: Purchase Decision

    Customer Goal:

    Select vendor.

    Touchpoints:

    • Proposal

    • ROI calculation

    • Contract

    Pain:

    High investment risk.

    Opportunity:

    Business case demonstration.

    Stage 4: Implementation

    Customer Goal:

    Deploy successfully.

    Touchpoints:

    • Training

    • Configuration

    • Data migration

    Pain:

    User adoption issues.

    Opportunity:

    Change management.

    Stage 5: Usage

    Customer Goal:

    Improve workflow.

    Touchpoints:

    • AI dashboard

    • Reports

    • Support

    Pain:

    Users need confidence in AI.

    Opportunity:

    Explainable AI.

    4. User Journey vs Customer Journey

    Very important.

    User Journey Customer Journey

    Focuses on product usage Focuses on entire relationship

    Usually one user Customer organization/person

    Shorter timeframe Long-term lifecycle

    Product interaction Business interaction

    Example:

    Customer Journey

    Pharma company buys AI platform.

    User Journey

    Safety reviewer uses AI platform daily.

    5. User Flow Design

    Now we go deeper into product interaction.

    Definition:

    User flow is the path a user follows to complete a specific task inside a digital product.

    It answers:

    \"How does the user complete an action?\"

    Example:

    User Goal:

    Generate AI summary.

    User Flow:

    Login

    ↓

    Open Case Dashboard

    ↓

    Select Case

    ↓

    Click Generate Summary

    ↓

    AI Processing

    ↓

    View Summary

    ↓

    Approve

    6. User Flow Components

    A user flow includes:

    Entry Point

    Where user starts.

    Example:

    Login screen.

    Actions

    What user does.

    Example:

    Click button.

    Screens

    Where interaction happens.

    Example:

    Dashboard.

    Decisions

    Choices user makes.

    Example:

    Approve or reject.

    Exit Point

    Task completion.

    Example:

    Summary approved.

    7. User Flow Example: AI Case Review

    Goal:

    Reviewer completes case review.

    Flow:

    Login

    ↓

    Dashboard

    ↓

    View Pending Cases

    ↓

    Select Case

    ↓

    Review AI Summary

    ↓

    Check Evidence

    ↓

    Decision

    |

    |

    Approve / Request Changes

    |

    |

    Complete

    8. User Flow vs Workflow

    Another common confusion.

    Workflow User Flow

    Business/system process User interaction path

    Includes backend activities Mostly user-facing

    Focus on operations Focus on usability

    Example:

    Workflow:

    Case Received

    ↓

    AI Processing

    ↓

    Medical Approval

    User Flow:

    Open Dashboard

    ↓

    Click Case

    ↓

    Review AI Summary

    ↓

    Approve

    9. Funnel Analysis

    A product BA also studies user movement.

    A funnel shows:

    How many users move from one stage to another.

    Example:

    AI Platform Adoption Funnel:

    1000 Users Invited

    ↓

    800 Login

    ↓

    600 Upload Cases

    ↓

    400 Use AI Summary

    ↓

    300 Approve Cases

    10. Drop-off Analysis

    Drop-off means users leave before completing a task.

    Example:

    Problem:

    Many reviewers stop using AI summary.

    BA investigates.

    Finds:

    Users don\'t trust AI recommendations.

    Requirement:

    Add:

    • Confidence score

    • Explanation

    • Evidence links

    11. Conversion Points

    Conversion point:

    The moment user completes a desired action.

    Examples:

    Healthcare App:

    • Patient books appointment

    • Doctor completes consultation

    • User subscribes

    AI Product:

    • Reviewer accepts AI suggestion

    • Team adopts workflow

    12. Customer Journey → Business Requirements

    Example:

    Customer Pain:

    \"Implementation takes too long.\"

    ↓

    Business Need:

    Faster onboarding.

    ↓

    Requirement:

    \"The system shall provide guided setup.\"

    13. User Flow → Functional Requirements

    Example:

    User Flow:

    Click \"Generate Summary\"

    ↓

    Requirement:

    \"The system shall generate an AI summary after document upload.\"

    A senior BA connects:

    Customer Journey

    ↓

    Identifies Business Problems

    User Journey

    ↓

    Identifies User Problems

    User Flow

    ↓

    Defines Product Interaction

    Requirements

    ↓

    Development

    16. Interview Questions

    Q1. Difference between customer journey and user journey?

    Answer:

    \"Customer journey covers the complete relationship with an organization, while user journey focuses on a user\'s interaction with a product or service.\"

    Q2. Difference between user flow and workflow?

    Answer:

    \"User flow describes the steps a user takes inside a product, while workflow describes the movement of work across people and systems.\"

    Q3. Why should BAs understand user flows?

    Answer:

    \"User flows help BAs define better functional requirements, improve usability, and collaborate effectively with UX and development teams.\"

    Lesson 37: Wireframing for BA / Product Roles Masterclass

    Translating Requirements into Visual Product Structure

    A requirement explains what the system should do. A wireframe explains how the user will interact with that functionality.

    A modern BA is not a UI designer, but a strong BA must understand wireframes because they help connect:

    Business Requirement → User Need → Screen Design → Development

    Learning Objectives

    By the end of this lesson, you will understand:

    • What is wireframing

    • Why BAs create/use wireframes

    • Wireframe types

    • Low fidelity vs high fidelity

    • Wireframe components

    • Screen requirement documentation

    • User interaction mapping

    • Collaboration with UX designers

    • Healthcare AI dashboard wireframe example

    1. What is a Wireframe?

    Definition:

    A wireframe is a visual blueprint of a digital product interface that shows the structure, layout, content placement, and user interactions before final design and development.

    Simple:

    A wireframe is the skeleton of an application screen.

    It does not focus on:

    • Colors

    • Fonts

    • Branding

    It focuses on:

    • What appears where

    • What users can do

    • How users navigate

    Example

    Requirement:

    \"Reviewer should see AI risk assessment.\"

    Without wireframe:

    Developer may create anything.

    With wireframe:

    --------------------------------

    Case Review Dashboard

    Patient Information

    Drug Information

    AI Risk Score

    [High Risk 85%]

    Evidence

    [Approve] [Request Review]

    --------------------------------

    2. Why Does a BA Need Wireframing?

    A BA uses wireframes to:

    ✅ Clarify requirements\ ✅ Reduce misunderstanding\ ✅ Validate user expectations\ ✅ Support stakeholder discussions\ ✅ Collaborate with UX designers\ ✅ Help developers understand screens

    3. Wireframe vs Prototype vs Mockup

    Important interview topic.

    Item Purpose Detail Level

    Wireframe Structure/layout Low

    Mockup Visual design Medium/High

    Prototype Interactive experience High

    Example:

    Wireframe

    Where button exists.

    Mockup

    What button looks like.

    Prototype

    How button behaves when clicked.

    4. Types of Wireframes

    There are three levels.

    Level 1: Low Fidelity Wireframe

    Also called:

    Sketch wireframe

    Purpose:

    Early thinking.

    Characteristics:

    • Simple boxes

    • No colors

    • Basic layout

    Example:

    ------------------

    Logo

    ------------------

    Search Box

    List

    Button

    ------------------

    Used during:

    • Requirement discussions

    • Brainstorming

    Level 2: Mid Fidelity Wireframe

    More detailed.

    Includes:

    • Real labels

    • Navigation

    • Data fields

    • Buttons

    Example:

    --------------------------------

    Case ID: PV-10245

    Status: Under Review

    AI Risk Score: 85%

    [View Evidence]

    [Approve Case]

    --------------------------------

    Level 3: High Fidelity Wireframe

    Almost final design.

    Includes:

    • Colors

    • Icons

    • Real content

    • Visual styling

    Usually created by UX designers.

    5. Wireframe Components

    A BA should understand:

    1. Layout

    Where information appears.

    Example:

    Dashboard:

    Header

    Navigation

    Main Content

    Sidebar

    Footer

    2. Navigation

    How users move.

    Example:

    Dashboard

    ↓

    Cases

    ↓

    Case Details

    ↓

    Approval

    3. Input Fields

    Example:

    Patient Report Form:

    • Patient ID

    • Drug Name

    • Event Description

    • Severity

    4. Actions

    Buttons:

    • Submit

    • Save

    • Approve

    • Reject

    5. Information Display

    Examples:

    • Tables

    • Cards

    • Charts

    • Notifications

    6. Wireframe Creation Process for BA

    A BA follows:

    Step 1: Understand User Goal

    Example:

    Reviewer wants:

    \"Quickly identify high-risk cases.\"

    Step 2: Identify Required Information

    Need:

    • Case ID

    • Patient details

    • Drug details

    • AI score

    • Evidence

    Step 3: Define User Actions

    User can:

    • Review

    • Approve

    • Reject

    • Escalate

    Step 4: Create Screen Structure

    Arrange:

    Information + Actions

    Step 5: Validate With Stakeholders

    Ask:

    \"Does this screen support your workflow?\"

    7. Screen Requirement Document

    A BA often documents screens.

    Example:

    Screen Name:

    Case Review Dashboard

    Purpose:

    Allow safety reviewers to analyze AI-generated case assessments.

    Users:

    Safety Reviewer

    Components:

    Component Purpose

    Case Summary Show patient/drug details

    Risk Score Display AI prediction

    Evidence Panel Explain AI decision

    Action Buttons Approve/reject

    8. Healthcare AI Wireframe Example

    Product:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

    Screen:

    AI Case Review Dashboard

    ================================================

    HEADER

    AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

    ================================================

    CASE INFORMATION

    Case ID: PV-45891

    Patient:

    Age:

    Medical History:

    ================================================

    AI ANALYSIS

    Risk Score

    ████████ 85%

    Risk Level:

    HIGH

    AI Explanation:

    - Serious adverse event detected

    - Previous similar cases found

    ================================================

    EVIDENCE

    Document 1

    Document 2

    ================================================

    ACTIONS

    [Approve]

    [Request Medical Review]

    [Reject]

    ================================================

    9. BA Role vs UX Designer Role

    Important distinction.

    BA Defines:

    • User needs

    • Business rules

    • Information required

    • Actions

    • Workflow

    UX Designer Defines:

    • Visual style

    • Interaction patterns

    • Layout improvements

    • User experience details

    Both collaborate.

    10. Wireframe and User Story Connection

    Example User Story:

    \"As a safety reviewer, I want to see AI risk scores so that I can prioritize critical cases.\"

    Wireframe identifies:

    Where:

    AI Risk Score Card

    appears.

    Acceptance Criteria:

    Given:

    Case is processed

    When:

    Reviewer opens case

    Then:

    Risk score should display.

    11. Common Wireframe Mistakes

    Mistake 1:

    Designing before understanding requirements.

    Mistake 2:

    Adding too much information.

    Mistake 3:

    Ignoring user workflow.

    Mistake 4:

    Not considering errors.

    Example:

    What happens if AI fails?

    12. Wireframe in Agile Environment

    Where wireframes are used:

    Backlog Refinement

    Explain upcoming feature.

    Sprint Planning

    Help developers understand UI needs.

    UX Review

    Validate experience.

    UAT

    Compare delivered screen with requirement.

    13. Interview Questions

    Q1. Why should a BA create wireframes?

    Answer:

    \"Wireframes help translate business requirements into visual structures, reduce ambiguity, and improve communication between stakeholders, UX designers, and developers.\"

    Q2. Difference between wireframe and prototype?

    Answer:

    \"A wireframe shows the structure of a screen, while a prototype demonstrates interactive behavior.\"

    Q3. Is BA responsible for final UI design?

    Answer:

    \"Usually no. BA defines requirements, workflows, and user needs. UX designers own detailed visual design.\"

    Practical Assignment

    Create a low-fidelity wireframe for:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Case Dashboard

    Include:

    Header

    Case Information Section

    AI Risk Score Section

    Evidence Section

    Action Buttons

    Also write:

    1. Screen purpose

    2. Users

    3. Functional requirements

    4. Acceptance criteria

    Lesson Summary

    Today you learned:

    ✅ Wireframing concepts\ ✅ Wireframe types\ ✅ BA role in UX collaboration\ ✅ Screen requirement documentation\ ✅ Dashboard design thinking\ ✅ Wireframe → User Story connection\ ✅ Healthcare AI example

    Lesson 17 -- Prototyping Techniques Masterclass

    From Wireframe to Interactive Product Experience

    1. What is Prototyping?

    Definition:

    A prototype is an interactive model of a product that demonstrates how users will interact with the system before actual development begins.

    Simple:

    Wireframe = Structure

    Prototype = Experience

    Example:

    Wireframe:

    Login Screen

    Username

    Password

    [Login Button]

    Prototype:

    User clicks:

    Enter username

    ↓

    Enter password

    ↓

    Click Login

    ↓

    Dashboard Opens

    The user can experience the flow.

    2. Why Does a BA Need Prototyping?

    A BA uses prototypes to:

    ✅ Validate requirements\ ✅ Get stakeholder feedback early\ ✅ Reduce development rework\ ✅ Identify missing requirements\ ✅ Explain user journeys clearly\ ✅ Improve collaboration with UX and developers

    Example:

    Requirement:

    \"The system should allow case approval.\"

    Stakeholder sees prototype and says:

    \"Before approval, we need an evidence review screen.\"

    This requirement would have been missed without prototype validation.

    3. Wireframe vs Prototype

    Wireframe Prototype

    Static structure Interactive model

    Shows screen layout Shows user behavior

    Early requirement discussion User testing

    No navigation Clickable navigation

    Example:

    Wireframe:

    Case Dashboard

    |

    |

    Approve Button

    Prototype:

    Dashboard

    ↓

    Click Approve

    ↓

    Confirmation Popup

    ↓

    Case Status Changed

    4. Types of Prototypes

    There are four common types.

    Type 1: Paper Prototype

    Lowest fidelity.

    Example:

    Hand-drawn screens.

    Used for:

    • Early ideas

    • Brainstorming

    Example:

    A rough sketch:

    ----------------

    Search Patient

    [ ]

    [Search]

    ----------------

    Type 2: Low Fidelity Prototype

    Simple digital prototype.

    Contains:

    • Basic screens

    • Navigation

    • Simple interactions

    Used for:

    • Requirement validation

    Example:

    AI Dashboard:

    Login

    ↓

    Case List

    ↓

    Case Detail

    ↓

    Approve

    Type 3: High Fidelity Prototype

    Near-real product experience.

    Contains:

    • Real screens

    • Colors

    • Data

    • Interactions

    Used for:

    • User testing

    • Stakeholder approval

    Type 4: Functional Prototype

    Almost working product.

    Includes:

    • Real data

    • APIs

    • Limited functionality

    Example:

    AI model generating sample risk scores.

    5. Prototype Development Process

    A BA/Product team follows:

    User Need

    ↓

    Requirements

    ↓

    Wireframe

    ↓

    Prototype

    ↓

    User Feedback

    ↓

    Requirement Refinement

    ↓

    Development

    6. Prototype Validation

    A prototype is not created just for presentation.

    It is tested.

    Questions:

    Can users complete the task?

    Example:

    Can a safety reviewer approve a case easily?

    Is information available?

    Example:

    Does reviewer see required evidence?

    Are decisions clear?

    Example:

    Approve vs Reject buttons.

    7. Prototype Testing Methods

    1. Stakeholder Review

    Business users review the prototype.

    Example:

    Pharmacovigilance manager reviews workflow.

    2. Usability Testing

    Real users try completing tasks.

    Example:

    \"Review this adverse event case.\"

    Observe:

    • Time taken

    • Confusion points

    • Errors

    3. A/B Testing

    Compare two designs.

    Example:

    Version A:

    Risk score on top.

    Version B:

    Risk score on sidebar.

    Measure:

    Which improves review speed?

    8. Figma Basics for BA

    A BA does not need to become a UI designer.

    But should understand:

    Frames

    Represent screens.

    Example:

    Login Screen

    Dashboard Screen

    Case Screen

    Components

    Reusable UI elements.

    Example:

    Button:

    [Approve]

    Used everywhere.

    Prototype Links

    Connect screens.

    Example:

    Login Frame

    ↓

    Dashboard Frame

    ↓

    Case Detail Frame

    9. Healthcare AI Prototype Example

    Product:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

    User:

    Safety Reviewer

    Goal:

    Review high-risk cases quickly.

    Prototype Flow:

    Login

    ↓

    Dashboard

    ↓

    High Risk Case List

    ↓

    Case Details

    ↓

    AI Summary

    ↓

    Evidence View

    ↓

    Risk Explanation

    ↓

    Approve / Escalate

    10. Prototype → User Story

    Prototype helps create better user stories.

    Example:

    Prototype shows:

    AI confidence score.

    User Story:

    As a safety reviewer, I want to see AI confidence scores so that I can decide whether additional review is required.

    Acceptance Criteria:

    Given:

    AI has processed a case

    When:

    Reviewer opens case details

    Then:

    Confidence score should be displayed.

    11. Prototype → Requirements

    Example:

    Prototype Finding:

    Reviewer needs evidence behind AI prediction.

    Requirement:

    \"The system shall display supporting evidence for AI-generated recommendations.\"

    12. Common BA Prototype Mistakes

    Mistake 1:

    Creating screens without understanding user goals.

    Mistake 2:

    Making prototype too detailed too early.

    Mistake 3:

    Ignoring edge cases.

    Example:

    What happens when AI fails?

    Mistake 4:

    Not involving users.

    13. Interview Questions

    Q1. Why create prototypes?

    Answer:

    \"Prototypes help validate requirements, gather feedback, reduce ambiguity, and ensure the final product meets user expectations.\"

    Q2. Difference between wireframe and prototype?

    Answer:

    \"Wireframes represent the structure of a screen, while prototypes simulate user interaction and product behavior.\"

    Q3. What is prototype validation?

    Answer:

    \"Prototype validation is testing the design with stakeholders or users to identify usability issues and refine requirements before development.\"

    Practical Assignment

    Create a prototype flow for:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Case Review

    Screens:

    1. Login

    2. Dashboard

    3. Case List

    4. Case Details

    5. AI Summary

    6. Risk Explanation

    7. Approval Screen

    For each screen define:

    • Purpose

    • User action

    • Requirement

    • Acceptance criteria

    PHASE 4 -- Product Design & UX Collaboration

    Lesson 18 -- UX Requirements & Design Collaboration Masterclass

    Bridging Business Requirements, User Experience & Product Design

    Module Progress Reminder

    Your roadmap:

    Total Lessons: 36

    Completed:

    ✅ Lesson 1--17

    Remaining:

    19 Lessons Left

    Current:

    ➡️ Lesson 18 of 36

    1. Why UX Matters for a Business Analyst

    Traditional BA thinking:

    \"The system should perform this function.\"

    Modern Product BA thinking:

    \"The system should help users achieve their goal easily, efficiently, and confidently.\"

    Example:

    Business Requirement:

    \"Create an AI risk scoring feature.\"

    Traditional requirement:

    System shall calculate risk score.

    UX-focused BA requirement:

    Safety reviewers should be able to understand case risk within seconds using an explainable AI risk score with supporting evidence.

    2. What Are UX Requirements?

    Definition:

    UX requirements describe how the product should behave from the user\'s perspective to provide an effective, efficient, and satisfying experience.

    They define:

    • Ease of use

    • User interaction

    • Navigation

    • Accessibility

    • User satisfaction

    • Error prevention

    3. Functional Requirement vs UX Requirement

    Very important BA concept.

    Functional Requirement UX Requirement

    What system does How user experiences it

    Feature behavior User interaction quality

    Business functionality Usability

    Example:

    Functional Requirement:

    \"The system shall allow users to upload safety reports.\"

    UX Requirement:

    \"Users should be able to upload a report within three steps with clear progress indicators.\"

    4. Types of UX Requirements

    A BA should understand these categories:

    1. Usability Requirements

    Focus:

    How easy is the system to use?

    Examples:

    • User should complete case review within 5 minutes.

    • Frequently used actions should be accessible from dashboard.

    2. Accessibility Requirements

    Focus:

    Can all users use the product?

    Examples:

    • Support keyboard navigation.

    • Provide readable text contrast.

    • Support screen readers.

    3. Navigation Requirements

    Focus:

    How users move through the system.

    Example:

    A reviewer should reach case details within two clicks.

    4. Interaction Requirements

    Focus:

    How users interact.

    Examples:

    • Confirmation before deleting data.

    • Show loading status during AI processing.

    5. Visual Requirements

    Focus:

    Information presentation.

    Examples:

    • Highlight critical risks.

    • Use consistent icons.

    6. Performance Experience Requirements

    Focus:

    User perception.

    Example:

    \"The system shall display loading status if AI processing takes more than 3 seconds.\"

    5. UX Principles Every BA Should Know

    Principle 1: User-Centered Design

    Design around user goals.

    Bad:

    \"We added 20 features.\"

    Good:

    \"We reduced reviewer effort.\"

    Principle 2: Reduce Cognitive Load

    Users should not think unnecessarily.

    Example:

    Before:

    Reviewer reads 20-page report.

    After:

    AI provides:

    • Summary

    • Important findings

    • Risk indicators

    Principle 3: Consistency

    Similar actions should look similar.

    Example:

    Approve button should appear consistently.

    Principle 4: Feedback

    System should tell users what is happening.

    Bad:

    Click button → nothing happens.

    Good:

    \"AI analysis in progress...\"

    Principle 5: Error Prevention

    Prevent mistakes before they happen.

    Example:

    Before submitting:

    \"Are you sure all required fields are completed?\"

    6. BA Collaboration With UX Designer

    A BA and UX designer work together.

    BA Provides:

    Business Context

    Why feature is needed.

    Example:

    Reduce safety review time.

    User Requirements

    Who needs it.

    Example:

    Safety reviewer.

    Business Rules

    Example:

    High-risk cases require medical approval.

    Acceptance Criteria

    Example:

    Risk score must be visible before approval.

    UX Designer Provides:

    User Interface Design

    • Layout

    • Visual style

    • Interaction patterns

    User Experience Decisions

    • Navigation

    • Screen hierarchy

    • User testing

    7. UX Design Collaboration Process

    Typical product workflow:

    Business Problem

    ↓

    BA Analysis

    ↓

    User Research

    ↓

    Requirements

    ↓

    Wireframe

    ↓

    Prototype

    ↓

    UX Review

    ↓

    Development

    ↓

    User Testing

    8. Design Review Meetings

    A BA participates in design reviews.

    Questions BA should ask:

    Does design support business goals?

    Example:

    Can reviewers identify high-risk cases quickly?

    Are all requirements covered?

    Example:

    Where is audit history shown?

    Are business rules reflected?

    Example:

    Does high-risk approval flow exist?

    Are edge cases handled?

    Example:

    What happens when AI confidence is low?

    9. UX Acceptance Criteria

    Traditional:

    System shall display risk score.

    Better:

    Given a processed case, when the reviewer opens case details, then the AI risk score should be displayed prominently with confidence level and explanation.

    UX criteria examples:

    Navigation

    Given user is on dashboard

    When user selects a case

    Then case details should open within one action.

    Error Handling

    Given AI processing fails

    When user opens the case

    Then system should show failure reason and manual review option.

    Feedback

    Given user uploads a report

    When processing starts

    Then system should display processing status.

    10. Healthcare AI Example

    Product:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

    UX Problem:

    Reviewers struggle to understand AI recommendations.

    BA Analysis:

    User Pain:

    \"I don\'t trust AI decisions.\"

    UX Requirement:

    System should provide:

    • Risk score

    • Confidence level

    • Supporting evidence

    • Explanation

    Screen Design:

    --------------------------------

    AI Risk Assessment

    Risk Level:

    HIGH

    Confidence:

    92%

    Reason:

    ✓ Serious adverse event detected

    ✓ Similar historical cases found

    Evidence:

    Document A

    Document B

    [Approve]

    [Request Review]

    --------------------------------

    11. UX Requirements Document Example

    A BA may document:

    Feature:

    AI Risk Dashboard

    User:

    Safety Reviewer

    Goal:

    Quickly identify serious cases.

    UX Requirements:

    UR-001:

    Dashboard should highlight high-risk cases.

    UR-002:

    User should understand AI reasoning.

    UR-003:

    Critical actions require confirmation.

    UR-004:

    System should show processing status.

    12. Common BA Mistakes in UX Collaboration

    Mistake 1:

    Only focusing on functionality.

    Mistake 2:

    Ignoring user emotions.

    Mistake 3:

    Giving solutions instead of understanding problems.

    Example:

    Wrong:

    \"Add another button.\"

    Correct:

    \"Why does the user need this action?\"

    Mistake 4:

    Not validating with users.

    13. Interview Questions

    Q1. What is the role of BA in UX design?

    Answer:

    \"A BA connects business objectives, user needs, and product requirements while collaborating with UX designers to create usable solutions.\"

    Q2. Difference between functional and UX requirements?

    Answer:

    \"Functional requirements define what the system does, while UX requirements define how effectively and easily users interact with the system.\"

    Q3. How do you ensure design meets requirements?

    Answer:

    \"Through design reviews, prototypes, acceptance criteria validation, and user feedback sessions.\"

    Practical Assignment

    For AI Pharmacovigilance Platform:

    Create UX Requirements for:

    Feature:

    AI Risk Assessment Dashboard

    Define:

    1. User goal

    2. User problem

    3. Functional requirements

    4. UX requirements

    5. Acceptance criteria

    Example:

    Functional:

    \"System shall display risk score.\"

    UX:

    \"Risk score should be visually prominent and understandable.\"

    ✅ UX requirements\ ✅ Functional vs UX requirements\ ✅ Usability requirements\ ✅ Accessibility requirements\ ✅ Navigation requirements\ ✅ UX collaboration process\ ✅ UX acceptance criteria\ ✅ Healthcare AI UX example

    Lesson 19 -- Design Thinking for Business Analysts Masterclass

    Human-Centered Problem Solving for BA & Product Roles

    Module Progress Reminder

    Your roadmap:

    Total Lessons: 36

    Completed:

    ✅ Lesson 1--18

    Current:

    ➡️ Lesson 19 of 36

    Remaining:

    17 Lessons Left

    1. What is Design Thinking?

    Definition:

    Design Thinking is a human-centered approach to solving problems by understanding users, defining the real problem, generating ideas, creating solutions, and testing them.

    Simple:

    Traditional approach:

    Business decides solution

    ↓

    Build product

    ↓

    Users adapt

    Design Thinking approach:

    Understand users

    ↓

    Find real problem

    ↓

    Create ideas

    ↓

    Prototype

    ↓

    Test with users

    ↓

    Build solution

    2. Why Does a BA Need Design Thinking?

    A traditional BA asks:

    \"What requirements should we collect?\"

    A Design Thinking BA asks:

    \"What problem are we solving and why does the user need it?\"

    Benefits for BA:

    ✅ Better requirement discovery\ ✅ Reduces wrong assumptions\ ✅ Improves user experience\ ✅ Finds hidden problems\ ✅ Creates innovative solutions

    3. Design Thinking Framework

    The most common model has 5 stages:

    1. Empathize

    ↓

    2. Define

    ↓

    3. Ideate

    ↓

    4. Prototype

    ↓

    5. Test

    PHASE 1: EMPATHIZE

    Understand the User

    Goal:

    Discover:

    • User problems

    • User needs

    • User behavior

    • User emotions

    BA techniques used:

    • Interviews

    • Observation

    • Surveys

    • Workshops

    • User journey mapping

    Example: Pharmacovigilance AI Platform

    Business says:

    \"We need AI automation.\"

    BA does not immediately create requirements.

    BA asks:

    Who uses it?

    Answer:

    Safety reviewers.

    Interview findings:

    Reviewer says:

    \"I spend 4 hours reading case documents.\"

    \"I struggle to identify serious risks.\"

    \"I don\'t trust automated decisions.\"

    Now we understand the real problem.

    Empathy Map

    A useful BA tool.

    It captures:

    THINKS

    |

    SAYS ---------------- DOES

    |

    FEELS

    Example:

    Safety Reviewer

    Says:

    \"I have too many cases.\"

    Thinks:

    \"Will AI miss important information?\"

    Does:

    Reads documents manually.

    Feels:

    Overwhelmed and cautious.

    PHASE 2: DEFINE

    Identify the Real Problem

    The BA converts observations into a problem statement.

    Bad Problem Statement:

    \"We need an AI dashboard.\"

    Why?

    Because it already assumes a solution.

    Good Problem Statement:

    \"Safety reviewers need a faster way to identify high-risk cases because manual review takes significant time and increases workload.\"

    Problem Statement Template

    [User]

    needs

    [Need]

    because

    [Reason/Pain]

    Example:

    Safety reviewers need quick risk identification because reviewing large volumes of reports manually is time-consuming.

    PHASE 3: IDEATE

    Generate Solutions

    At this stage:

    Do not immediately choose one solution.

    Generate multiple ideas.

    Techniques:

    1. Brainstorming

    Team generates ideas.

    2. Mind Mapping

    Connect related ideas.

    3. Crazy 8

    Create 8 ideas quickly.

    4. \"How Might We\" Questions

    Very important.

    Example:

    Problem:

    Reviewers spend too much time reading reports.

    How Might We:

    \"How might we reduce the time needed to understand safety cases?\"

    Possible ideas:

    1. AI summary

    2. Risk highlighting

    3. Automatic classification

    4. Smart search

    PHASE 4: PROTOTYPE

    Create Solution Representation

    Prototype helps test ideas.

    Examples:

    • Wireframes

    • Clickable prototypes

    • Mockups

    Example:

    AI Review Dashboard prototype:

    Case Summary

    ↓

    AI Risk Score

    ↓

    Evidence Explanation

    ↓

    Approval Decision

    PHASE 5: TEST

    Validate With Users

    Goal:

    Learn:

    • Does it solve the problem?

    • Is it easy to use?

    • What needs improvement?

    Example:

    Reviewer tests prototype.

    Feedback:

    \"Risk score is useful, but I need evidence.\"

    BA updates requirements:

    Add evidence explanation.

    4. Design Thinking vs Traditional BA Approach

    Traditional BA Design Thinking BA

    Requirement focused Problem focused

    Collects stated needs Finds hidden needs

    Starts with solution Starts with user

    Documents requirements Validates assumptions

    5. Design Thinking Tools for BA

    1. Empathy Map

    Understand users.

    2. Customer Journey Map

    Understand experience.

    3. Persona

    Represent users.

    4. Problem Statement

    Define the challenge.

    5. User Story

    Convert needs into requirements.

    6. Prototype

    Validate solution.

    6. Healthcare AI Case Study

    Problem:

    Pharmacovigilance team receives 50,000 reports yearly.

    Empathize Findings:

    Users:

    • Spend hours reading reports

    • Miss important signals

    • Need confidence before decisions

    Define:

    \"Safety reviewers need AI assistance to prioritize and analyze cases faster.\"

    Ideate:

    Solutions:

    • AI extraction

    • Risk scoring

    • Similar case detection

    • Automated summaries

    Prototype:

    AI dashboard.

    Test:

    Reviewers validate workflow.

    Final Product:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Copilot.

    7. Design Thinking Workshop Example

    A BA may conduct:

    Workshop Duration:

    2 hours

    Participants:

    • Product Manager

    • Users

    • UX Designer

    • Developers

    • Compliance Team

    Agenda:

    Step 1:

    Understand users

    30 min

    Step 2:

    Define problems

    30 min

    Step 3:

    Generate solutions

    30 min

    Step 4:

    Select prototype idea

    30 min

    8. Converting Design Thinking Output Into BA Documents

    Important connection:

    Design Thinking

    ↓

    Problem Statement

    ↓

    Business Requirement

    ↓

    BRD

    ↓

    Functional Requirement

    ↓

    User Stories

    ↓

    Acceptance Criteria

    ↓

    Development

    9. Interview Questions

    Q1. What is Design Thinking?

    Answer:

    \"Design Thinking is a human-centered problem-solving approach that focuses on understanding users, defining problems, creating solutions, prototyping, and testing.\"

    Q2. Why should a BA use Design Thinking?

    Answer:

    \"It helps BAs discover the real business problem, understand user needs, and create solutions that provide better value.\"

    Q3. Difference between brainstorming and ideation?

    Answer:

    \"Brainstorming is one technique used during ideation. Ideation is the broader process of generating and exploring possible solutions.\"

    Practical Assignment

    For AI Pharmacovigilance Platform:

    • Goals

    • Pain points

    • Frustrations

    2. Define

    3. Ideate

    Generate 5 possible solutions.

    4. Prototype

    Choose one feature.

    5. Test

    Define 5 user feedback questions.

    PHASE 5 -- Requirements Engineering Masterclass

    Lesson 20 -- Requirement Elicitation Techniques Masterclass

    Discovering the Real Business Needs Before Writing Requirements

    Module Progress Reminder

    Your roadmap:

    Total Lessons: 36

    Completed:

    ✅ Lesson 1--19

    Current:

    ➡️ Lesson 20 of 36

    Remaining:

    16 Lessons Left

    1. What is Requirement Elicitation?

    Definition:

    Requirement elicitation is the process of discovering, collecting, understanding, and documenting the needs, expectations, and constraints of stakeholders.

    Simple:

    Requirement elicitation means:

    Finding out what people actually need before building the solution.

    Example:

    Stakeholder says:

    \"We need an AI dashboard.\"

    A junior BA writes:

    \"Create dashboard.\"

    A senior BA asks:

    • Who will use it?

    • What problem does it solve?

    • What decisions will they make?

    • What information do they need?

    • What success looks like?

    2. Why is Requirement Elicitation Important?

    Poor requirements cause:

    ❌ Wrong product\ ❌ Rework\ ❌ Increased cost\ ❌ Delayed delivery\ ❌ User dissatisfaction

    Good elicitation creates:

    ✅ Clear scope\ ✅ Better user stories\ ✅ Better BRD/FRD\ ✅ Better acceptance criteria\ ✅ Higher product success

    3. Requirement Elicitation vs Requirement Gathering

    Important BA concept.

    Many people use these terms interchangeably.

    Requirement Gathering

    Means:

    Collecting already known information.

    Example:

    Stakeholder provides existing process documents.

    Requirement Elicitation

    Means:

    Discovering hidden needs.

    Example:

    Through interviews, BA discovers:

    \"Reviewers struggle because they cannot see historical cases.\"

    A senior BA focuses more on elicitation.

    4. Requirement Elicitation Process

    A professional BA follows:

    1. Identify Stakeholders

    ↓

    2. Understand Business Problem

    ↓

    3. Select Elicitation Technique

    ↓

    4. Conduct Sessions

    ↓

    5. Analyze Information

    ↓

    6. Document Requirements

    ↓

    7. Validate Requirements

    5. Requirement Elicitation Techniques

    The BABOK framework identifies many techniques.

    The most important for industry BAs are:

    1. Interviews

    2. Workshops

    3. Observation

    4. Surveys

    5. Document Analysis

    6. Brainstorming

    7. Focus Groups

    8. Prototyping

    9. Interface Analysis

    10. Reverse Engineering

    Technique 1: Stakeholder Interviews

    What is it?

    One-to-one discussion with stakeholders to understand needs.

    When to use?

    Best for:

    • Complex requirements

    • Expert knowledge

    • Sensitive topics

    Example:

    Interview:

    Pharmacovigilance Manager

    Questions:

    • How are cases currently reviewed?

    • What are major delays?

    • What decisions require expert judgment?

    • What reports are required?

    BA Interview Questions Framework

    Current State

    \"How do you perform this activity today?\"

    Problems

    \"What challenges do you face?\"

    Future State

    \"What would improve this process?\"

    Success

    \"How will we measure improvement?\"

    Technique 2: Workshops

    What is it?

    A group session where multiple stakeholders collaborate.

    Participants:

    • Business users

    • Product manager

    • Developers

    • Compliance

    • UX designer

    Example:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Workshop:

    Goal:

    Define AI case review workflow.

    Discussion:

    Current:

    Manual Review

    ↓

    Medical Assessment

    ↓

    Approval

    Future:

    AI Analysis

    ↓

    Reviewer Validation

    ↓

    Approval

    Workshop Benefits

    ✅ Faster requirement discovery\ ✅ Resolves conflicts\ ✅ Creates shared understanding

    Technique 3: Observation

    What is it?

    Watching users perform real tasks.

    Why important?

    Users may not explain everything.

    Example:

    BA observes safety reviewer.

    Finds:

    Reviewer uses:

    • Excel sheets

    • Email

    • Multiple systems

    Problem discovered:

    Too much manual switching.

    Requirement:

    \"System should provide centralized case information.\"

    Technique 4: Surveys

    Used when many users need feedback.

    Example:

    Healthcare app survey:

    Questions:

    • How often do you review cases?

    • Biggest challenges?

    • Which features are most useful?

    Advantages:

    • Large audience

    • Quick data collection

    Disadvantages:

    • Less detailed answers

    Technique 5: Document Analysis

    BA reviews existing documents.

    Examples:

    • SOPs

    • Policies

    • Regulations

    • Reports

    • Existing system manuals

    Healthcare example:

    Review:

    Pharmacovigilance SOP

    Find:

    • Reporting timelines

    • Approval rules

    • Compliance requirements

    Technique 6: Brainstorming

    Used for generating ideas.

    Example:

    Problem:

    Reduce manual review time.

    Ideas:

    • AI summary

    • Automation

    • Smart search

    • Classification

    Technique 7: Prototyping

    Prototype can also be an elicitation technique.

    Why?

    Users react better to visuals.

    Example:

    Show dashboard prototype.

    User says:

    \"We also need confidence score.\"

    New requirement discovered.

    Technique 8: Interface Analysis

    Used when systems integrate.

    Example:

    AI platform connects with:

    • Drug database

    • EHR system

    • Regulatory portal

    BA studies:

    • Data exchange

    • APIs

    • System limitations

    Technique 9: Reverse Engineering

    Used when documentation is missing.

    Example:

    Old legacy system.

    BA studies:

    • Existing screens

    • Database

    • Reports

    to understand requirements.

    6. Choosing the Right Technique

    Situation Best Technique

    Need expert knowledge Interview

    Multiple stakeholder conflict Workshop

    Unknown user problems Observation

    Large user group Survey

    Existing process exists Document Analysis

    Need user feedback Prototype

    Old system replacement Reverse Engineering

    7. Healthcare AI Case Study

    Problem:

    Pharma company wants AI safety platform.

    Step 1: Stakeholder Identification

    Stakeholders:

    • Safety reviewers

    • Medical reviewers

    • Regulatory team

    • IT team

    • Compliance team

    Step 2: Elicitation Plan

    Stakeholder Technique

    Safety Reviewer Interview + Observation

    Medical Team Workshop

    Compliance Document Analysis

    End Users Prototype Testing

    Step 3: Findings

    Business Need:

    Reduce review time.

    User Pain:

    Manual document analysis.

    Requirement:

    AI should extract important safety information.

    8. Requirement Validation

    After elicitation:

    Never directly document and send.

    Validate.

    Validation Questions:

    • Is requirement clear?

    • Is it complete?

    • Is it feasible?

    • Does it solve the problem?

    • Is priority correct?

    9. Common BA Mistakes

    Mistake 1:

    Accepting stakeholder request as requirement.

    Example:

    \"We need mobile app.\"

    BA should ask:

    \"Why?\"

    Mistake 2:

    Talking only to managers.

    Users are equally important.

    Mistake 3:

    Not documenting assumptions.

    Mistake 4:

    Not validating requirements.

    10. Interview Questions

    Q1. What is requirement elicitation?

    Answer:

    \"Requirement elicitation is the process of discovering stakeholder needs, analyzing business problems, and identifying requirements before solution development.\"

    Q2. Difference between interview and workshop?

    Answer:

    \"Interview is usually one-to-one information gathering, while workshops involve multiple stakeholders collaborating to define requirements.\"

    Q3. Why is observation useful?

    Answer:

    \"Observation reveals actual user behavior and hidden problems that users may not communicate during interviews.\"

    Practical Assignment

    For AI Pharmacovigilance Platform:

    Create an Elicitation Plan:

    Stakeholders:

    1. Safety Reviewer

    2. Medical Reviewer

    3. Compliance Manager

    4. IT Architect

    For each define:

    • Elicitation technique

    • Questions to ask

    • Expected requirements

    Lesson 21 -- BRD (Business Requirement Document) Masterclass

    How a Professional Business Analyst Creates a BRD

    Module Progress Reminder

    Your roadmap:

    Total Lessons: 36

    Completed:

    ✅ Lesson 1--20

    Current:

    ➡️ Lesson 21 of 36

    Remaining:

    15 Lessons Left

    1. What is a BRD?

    Definition

    A Business Requirement Document (BRD) describes the business problem, objectives, expected business outcomes, scope, and high-level requirements of a project.

    Simple:

    BRD answers:

    \"Why are we building this solution?\"

    A BRD focuses on:

    • Business goals

    • Problems

    • Expected outcomes

    • Stakeholder needs

    • Scope

    It does not focus on technical implementation.

    2. Where Does BRD Come in a Project?

    A typical flow:

    Business Problem

    ↓

    Business Analysis

    ↓

    BRD

    ↓

    FRD

    ↓

    SRS

    ↓

    User Stories

    ↓

    Development

    ↓

    Testing

    ↓

    Release

    3. BRD vs FRD vs SRS

    Very important interview topic.

    Document Main Question Audience

    BRD Why do we need this? Business stakeholders

    FRD What should the system do? BA + Developers

    SRS How should the system behave in detail? Technical teams

    Example:

    Business Problem:

    \"Safety reviewers spend too much time reviewing adverse event cases.\"

    BRD:

    \"We need AI automation to reduce review time by 50%.\"

    FRD:

    \"The system shall generate AI summaries from uploaded reports.\"

    SRS:

    \"The system shall process PDF documents, extract entities, and generate summary output within defined performance limits.\"

    4. Why is BRD Important?

    A good BRD provides:

    ✅ Clear business direction\ ✅ Stakeholder alignment\ ✅ Project scope definition\ ✅ Business justification\ ✅ Success measurement

    Without BRD:

    Different teams may have different expectations.

    5. Who Uses BRD?

    Business Stakeholders

    Understand:

    • Why project exists

    • Expected benefits

    Product Manager

    Uses it for:

    • Product vision

    • Roadmap planning

    BA

    Uses it for:

    • Requirement analysis

    Development Team

    Uses it for:

    • Understanding business context

    QA Team

    Uses it for:

    • Understanding business objectives

    6. BRD Structure (Industry Format)

    A professional BRD usually contains:

    1. Document Information

    Contains:

    • Document name

    • Version

    • Author

    • Date

    • Approval status

    Example:

    Document:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Platform BRD

    Version:

    1.0

    Author:

    Business Analyst

    2. Executive Summary

    Explains:

    • Project overview

    • Business reason

    Example:

    The pharmaceutical company wants to implement an AI-powered pharmacovigilance platform to improve adverse event processing efficiency and reduce manual review effort.

    3. Business Problem Statement

    Very important section.

    Template:

    Current Situation:

    Problem:

    Impact:

    Need:

    Example:

    Current Situation:

    Safety teams manually review thousands of adverse event reports.

    Problem:

    Review process is slow and inconsistent.

    Impact:

    • Delayed reporting

    • Increased operational cost

    • Reviewer workload

    Need:

    AI-assisted case processing platform.

    4. Business Objectives

    Objectives should be measurable.

    Bad:

    \"Improve efficiency.\"

    Good:

    \"Reduce average case review time by 40% within six months.\"

    Example:

    Objective Measurement

    Reduce review time 40% reduction

    Improve accuracy Reduce errors by 30%

    Improve compliance Faster reporting

    5. Project Scope

    Defines boundaries.

    In Scope

    Included:

    ✅ AI case summarization\ ✅ Risk prediction\ ✅ Case prioritization\ ✅ Reporting dashboard

    Out of Scope

    Not included:

    ❌ Drug discovery\ ❌ Clinical trial management\ ❌ Patient diagnosis

    6. Stakeholder Identification

    Example:

    Stakeholder Role

    Safety Manager Business owner

    Reviewer End user

    Compliance Team Regulatory validation

    IT Team Technical support

    7. Current State Analysis (AS-IS)

    Describe current process.

    Example:

    Current workflow:

    Report Received

    ↓

    Manual Data Entry

    ↓

    Reviewer Analysis

    ↓

    Medical Review

    ↓

    Submission

    Problems:

    • Manual effort

    • Duplicate work

    • Delays

    8. Future State Vision (TO-BE)

    Expected future process.

    Report Received

    ↓

    AI Extraction

    ↓

    AI Risk Analysis

    ↓

    Reviewer Validation

    ↓

    Submission

    Benefits:

    • Faster processing

    • Better prioritization

    • Improved consistency

    9. Business Requirements

    These are high-level needs.

    Format:

    BR-001:

    \"The business requires an AI-based solution to automatically analyze safety reports.\"

    Example:

    ID Requirement

    BR-001 Reduce manual review effort

    BR-002 Improve case prioritization

    BR-003 Maintain regulatory compliance

    10. Assumptions and Constraints

    Assumptions

    Things expected to be true.

    Example:

    • Users will receive training.

    • Data will be available.

    Constraints

    Limitations.

    Example:

    • Regulatory restrictions

    • Data privacy requirements

    • Budget limitations

    11. Success Criteria

    How do we know project succeeded?

    Example:

    Success:

    • 50% faster review process

    • 95% system availability

    • User satisfaction above 85%

    12. Risks

    Example:

    Risk Impact Mitigation

    Low AI accuracy High Human validation

    Data privacy issue High Security controls

    User resistance Medium Training

    Complete BRD Example

    Project:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

    Business Problem:

    Safety reviewers manually analyze large volumes of adverse event reports causing delays.

    Business Objective:

    Create an AI-assisted platform to reduce case review time and improve regulatory compliance.

    Scope:

    In Scope:

    • AI document extraction

    • Risk scoring

    • Review dashboard

    • Reporting support

    Out of Scope:

    • Medical diagnosis

    • Drug development

    Stakeholders:

    • Pharmacovigilance team

    • Medical reviewers

    • Compliance department

    Expected Benefits:

    • Faster review

    • Better accuracy

    • Reduced workload

    13. BRD Writing Tips for BA

    Tip 1:

    Always start with \"Why\".

    Tip 2:

    Avoid technical language.

    Wrong:

    \"Use NLP transformer model.\"

    Correct:

    \"System should automatically extract safety information from reports.\"

    Tip 3:

    Focus on business value.

    Tip 4:

    Make objectives measurable.

    14. Common BRD Mistakes

    Mistake 1:

    Writing features instead of business needs.

    Wrong:

    \"Add AI dashboard.\"

    Correct:

    \"Improve visibility of case risk information.\"

    Mistake 2:

    No scope definition.

    Mistake 3:

    No success criteria.

    Mistake 4:

    Mixing technical design into BRD.

    15. Interview Questions

    Q1. What is the purpose of BRD?

    Answer:

    \"BRD defines business objectives, problems, scope, and high-level requirements to align stakeholders before solution development.\"

    Q2. Difference between BRD and FRD?

    Answer:

    \"BRD explains business needs and objectives, while FRD describes the functional behavior required from the system.\"

    Q3. Who prepares BRD?

    Answer:

    \"Usually a Business Analyst prepares BRD by collaborating with business stakeholders, product owners, and subject matter experts.\"

    Practical Assignment

    Create a BRD outline for:

    Project:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

    Include:

    1. Executive Summary

    2. Business Problem

    3. Objectives

    4. Scope

    5. Stakeholders

    6. Current State

    7. Future State

    8. Business Requirements

    9. Success Criteria

    10. Risks

    Today you learned:

    ✅ BRD purpose\ ✅ BRD structure\ ✅ BRD vs FRD vs SRS\ ✅ Business problem documentation\ ✅ Scope definition\ ✅ Stakeholder analysis\ ✅ AS-IS and TO-BE\ ✅ Business requirements\ ✅ Real healthcare AI BRD example

    PHASE 5 -- Requirements Engineering Masterclass

    Lesson 22 -- FRD (Functional Requirement Document) Masterclass

    Converting Business Needs into System Functional Requirements

    Module Progress Reminder

    Your roadmap:

    Total Lessons: 36

    Completed:

    ✅ Lesson 1--21

    Current:

    ➡️ Lesson 22 of 36

    Remaining:

    14 Lessons Left

    1. What is FRD?

    Definition

    A Functional Requirement Document (FRD) describes what the system must do, the functions it must provide, and how users interact with those functions.

    Simple:

    BRD asks:

    \"Why are we building this?\"

    FRD asks:

    \"What should the system do?\"

    Example:

    BRD:

    \"Reduce pharmacovigilance case review time.\"

    FRD:

    \"The system shall automatically generate a summary of uploaded adverse event reports.\"

    2. Position of FRD in Software Development

    The flow:

    Business Need

    ↓

    BRD

    (Why)

    ↓

    FRD

    (What)

    ↓

    SRS

    (How in detail)

    ↓

    Development

    ↓

    Testing

    3. BRD vs FRD Difference

    Important interview question.

    BRD FRD

    Business perspective System perspective

    Explains goals Explains functionality

    High-level Detailed

    Written for business stakeholders Used by developers & testers

    Example:

    Business Requirement:

    \"Improve adverse event processing.\"

    Functional Requirements:

    FR-001:

    System shall allow users to upload adverse event reports.

    FR-002:

    System shall extract patient and drug information.

    FR-003:

    System shall generate risk classification.

    4. Why is FRD Important?

    A good FRD helps:

    ✅ Developers understand features\ ✅ QA create test cases\ ✅ Stakeholders validate functionality\ ✅ Reduce ambiguity\ ✅ Control scope

    5. Who Uses FRD?

    Business Analyst

    Creates and maintains FRD.

    Product Manager

    Ensures alignment with product goals.

    Developers

    Use it to build functionality.

    QA Engineers

    Convert requirements into test scenarios.

    UX Designers

    Understand user interactions.

    6. FRD Structure (Industry Template)

    A professional FRD contains:

    1. Document Information

    Example:

    Document:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Platform FRD

    Version:

    1.0

    Prepared By:

    Business Analyst

    2. Introduction

    Contains:

    • Purpose

    • Background

    • Scope

    Example:

    This document defines functional requirements for an AI-based pharmacovigilance system that supports adverse event processing and review.

    3. System Overview

    Describe major modules.

    Example:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Platform:

    Modules:

    1. Case Management

    2. AI Analysis Engine

    3. Review Dashboard

    4. Reporting Module

    5. User Management

    4. User Roles

    Define who interacts with system.

    Example:

    Role Responsibility

    Safety Reviewer Review cases

    Medical Reviewer Validate decisions

    Admin Manage users

    Compliance Officer Regulatory monitoring

    5. Functional Requirements

    Main section.

    Format:

    Requirement ID

    Description

    Priority

    User Role

    Acceptance Criteria

    Example:

    FR-001

    Requirement:

    The system shall allow safety reviewers to upload adverse event reports.

    Priority:

    High

    User:

    Safety Reviewer

    Acceptance Criteria:

    Given:

    User is logged in

    When:

    User uploads PDF report

    Then:

    System stores the document successfully.

    7. Functional Requirement Categories

    A BA usually organizes requirements by module.

    Module 1: User Authentication

    FR-001

    System shall allow users to login using authorized credentials.

    FR-002

    System shall lock account after multiple failed attempts.

    Module 2: Case Management

    FR-003

    System shall allow users to create a new safety case.

    FR-004

    System shall assign unique case ID automatically.

    Module 3: AI Analysis

    FR-005

    System shall extract safety information from uploaded documents.

    FR-006

    System shall generate AI risk assessment.

    Module 4: Review Dashboard

    FR-007

    System shall display pending cases.

    FR-008

    System shall allow reviewers to approve or reject cases.

    Module 5: Reporting

    FR-009

    System shall generate regulatory reports.

    8. Business Rules in FRD

    Business rules define decisions.

    Example:

    Rule:

    High-risk cases require medical approval.

    Document:

    BR-001:

    IF risk score > 80%

    THEN

    Medical reviewer approval required.

    Other examples:

    • Mandatory fields

    • Approval rules

    • Validation rules

    • Eligibility criteria

    9. Use Cases in FRD

    Many FRDs include use cases.

    Example:

    Use Case:

    Generate AI Risk Assessment

    Actor:

    Safety Reviewer

    Pre-condition:

    Case document uploaded.

    Main Flow:

    1. User opens case.

    2. User clicks \"Generate Assessment\".

    3. AI analyzes document.

    4. System displays risk score.

    Alternative Flow:

    AI processing fails.

    System shows error message.

    10. FRD Example: AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

    Feature:

    AI Case Summarization

    Functional Requirement:

    FR-AI-001

    The system shall generate a summary from uploaded adverse event documents.

    User:

    Safety Reviewer

    Input:

    PDF safety report

    Process:

    AI extracts:

    • Patient information

    • Drug information

    • Adverse event details

    Output:

    Summary displayed on dashboard.

    Acceptance Criteria:

    Given a valid document

    When user selects \"Generate Summary\"

    Then system displays AI-generated summary.

    11. FRD Writing Best Practices

    1. Use \"The system shall\"

    Professional standard.

    Example:

    \"The system shall display case status.\"

    2. Avoid vague words

    Avoid:

    • Fast

    • Easy

    • User-friendly

    Instead:

    Define measurable criteria.

    Bad:

    \"The system shall load quickly.\"

    Good:

    \"The system shall display dashboard within 3 seconds.\"

    3. One requirement = One behavior

    Bad:

    \"The system shall upload documents and analyze and approve cases.\"

    Too many functions.

    Better:

    FR-001 Upload document.

    FR-002 Analyze document.

    FR-003 Approve case.

    12. Common FRD Mistakes

    Mistake 1:

    Writing business goals instead of functions.

    Wrong:

    \"Improve compliance.\"

    Correct:

    \"System shall generate regulatory submission report.\"

    Mistake 2:

    Missing acceptance criteria.

    Mistake 3:

    Not defining user roles.

    Mistake 4:

    Combining multiple requirements.

    13. BRD → FRD Conversion Example

    BRD Statement:

    \"Reduce manual pharmacovigilance review effort.\"

    ↓

    BA Analysis:

    Need automation.

    ↓

    FRD Requirements:

    FR-001:

    System shall extract data from safety reports.

    FR-002:

    System shall generate AI summary.

    FR-003:

    System shall highlight critical findings.

    FR-004:

    System shall prioritize high-risk cases.

    14. Interview Questions

    Q1. What is FRD?

    Answer:

    \"FRD describes the functional behavior of a system, including features, user interactions, business rules, and expected outputs.\"

    Q2. Difference between BRD and FRD?

    Answer:

    \"BRD defines business objectives and needs, while FRD translates those needs into system functionality.\"

    Q3. Who creates FRD?

    Answer:

    \"Typically the Business Analyst creates FRD by collaborating with stakeholders, product owners, UX teams, and technical teams.\"

    Practical Assignment

    Prepare an FRD section for:

    Feature:

    AI Risk Assessment Dashboard

    Create:

    1. Functional Requirements (minimum 5)

    2. User Roles

    3. Business Rules

    4. Acceptance Criteria

    Example:

    FR-001:

    System shall display AI risk score.

    Acceptance:

    Given case is analyzed,

    When reviewer opens case,

    Then risk score is displayed.

    ✅ FRD purpose\ ✅ BRD vs FRD\ ✅ FRD structure\ ✅ Functional requirement writing\ ✅ Business rules\ ✅ Use cases\ ✅ Acceptance criteria connection\ ✅ Healthcare AI FRD example

    PHASE 5 -- Requirements Engineering Masterclass

    Lesson 23 -- SRS (Software Requirement Specification) Masterclass

    Converting Functional Requirements into Detailed System Specifications

    Module Progress Reminder

    Your roadmap:

    Total Lessons: 36

    Completed:

    ✅ Lesson 1--22

    Current:

    ➡️ Lesson 23 of 36

    Remaining:

    13 Lessons Left

    1. What is SRS?

    Definition

    Software Requirement Specification (SRS) is a detailed document that describes the complete behavior, functionality, constraints, interfaces, and quality requirements of a software system.

    Simple:

    BRD:

    Why are we building it?

    FRD:

    What should the system do?

    SRS:

    How exactly should the software behave?

    2. Requirement Documentation Hierarchy

    Professional BA flow:

    Business Problem

    ↓

    BRD

    (Business Need)

    ↓

    FRD

    (System Functions)

    ↓

    SRS

    (Detailed Specification)

    ↓

    User Stories

    (Agile Execution)

    ↓

    Development

    ↓

    Testing

    3. BRD vs FRD vs SRS

    Very important interview topic.

    Document Main Question Detail Level

    BRD Why do we need this? High-level

    FRD What functionality is needed? Medium

    SRS How should the software behave? Detailed

    Healthcare AI Example

    Business Requirement:

    \"Reduce manual safety review effort.\"

    BRD:

    \"The organization needs AI assistance to improve pharmacovigilance operations.\"

    FRD:

    \"The system shall generate AI summaries for safety cases.\"

    SRS:

    \"The system shall accept PDF, DOCX, and XML files up to 20MB, extract safety information, generate a summary within 30 seconds, and display confidence scores.\"

    4. Why is SRS Important?

    SRS helps:

    ✅ Developers understand exact expectations\ ✅ QA create detailed test cases\ ✅ Architects design solutions\ ✅ Stakeholders validate scope\ ✅ Reduce requirement ambiguity

    5. Who Uses SRS?

    Business Analyst

    Creates and manages requirements.

    Product Manager

    Ensures product alignment.

    Developers

    Use specifications for implementation.

    QA Engineers

    Create test scenarios.

    Architects

    Design technical solution.

    6. SRS Structure (Industry Template)

    A professional SRS contains:

    Section 1: Introduction

    Includes:

    • Purpose

    • Scope

    • Definitions

    • References

    Example:

    Purpose

    This document defines software requirements for an AI-powered pharmacovigilance platform.

    Section 2: Product Overview

    Describes:

    • System background

    • Product vision

    • User groups

    Example:

    System:

    AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

    Purpose:

    Assist safety teams in analyzing adverse event reports.

    Section 3: User Roles and Permissions

    Defines access.

    Example:

    Role Access

    Safety Reviewer Review cases

    Medical Reviewer Approve high-risk cases

    Admin Manage users

    Section 4: Functional Requirements

    Detailed system behavior.

    Example:

    SRS-FR-001

    Feature:

    Document Upload

    Requirement:

    The system shall allow safety reviewers to upload adverse event documents.

    Input:

    PDF, DOCX files

    Validation:

    Maximum size: 20 MB

    Output:

    Document stored successfully.

    Section 5: Non-Functional Requirements

    Defines quality expectations.

    Examples:

    • Performance

    • Security

    • Reliability

    • Scalability

    Example:

    Performance

    \"The dashboard shall load within 3 seconds.\"

    Security

    \"User authentication shall use multi-factor authentication.\"

    Availability

    \"The system shall maintain 99.5% availability.\"

    Section 6: External Interface Requirements

    Defines system interactions.

    Types:

    User Interface

    Example:

    Dashboard screens.

    API Interface

    Example:

    Connect with regulatory reporting system.

    Database Interface

    Example:

    Store case information.

    External Systems

    Example:

    Drug database integration.

    Section 7: Data Requirements

    Defines information handled by system.

    Example:

    Case Entity

    Fields:

    • Case ID

    • Patient ID

    • Drug Name

    • Event Description

    • Risk Level

    Section 8: Business Rules

    Example:

    Rule:

    High-risk cases require medical approval.

    Specification:

    IF Risk Score >= 80%

    THEN

    Medical Review Required

    Section 9: Assumptions and Constraints

    Assumptions:

    • Users have valid accounts.

    • Required data is available.

    Constraints:

    • Regulatory compliance requirements.

    • Data privacy restrictions.

    Section 10: Acceptance Criteria

    Defines when requirement is complete.

    Example:

    Requirement:

    AI Risk Score Display

    Acceptance Criteria:

    Given:

    Case analysis is completed.

    When:

    Reviewer opens case.

    Then:

    Risk score and explanation are displayed.

    7. SRS Example -- AI Pharmacovigilance Platform

    Feature:

    AI Risk Assessment

    Functional Specification

    Requirement:

    The system shall analyze safety cases using AI models.

    Input:

    • Patient information

    • Drug information

    • Medical event details

    Processing:

    AI analyzes:

    • Severity

    • Historical patterns

    • Similar cases

    Output:

    Display:

    • Risk score

    • Risk category

    • Explanation

    Performance Requirement:

    Response should be available within 30 seconds.

    Security Requirement:

    Only authorized reviewers can access case information.

    8. SRS and Agile Projects

    In traditional projects:

    SRS is created before development.

    In Agile:

    Large SRS may be replaced by:

    • Epics

    • User Stories

    • Acceptance Criteria

    • Backlog items

    But enterprise projects still use SRS.

    Examples:

    • Healthcare

    • Banking

    • Government

    • Regulatory systems

    9. SRS Writing Rules for BA

    Rule 1:

    Be precise.

    Bad:

    \"The system should be fast.\"

    Good:

    \"The system shall respond within 3 seconds.\"

    Rule 2:

    Avoid assumptions.

    Bad:

    \"Users know the process.\"

    Good:

    \"System shall provide onboarding instructions.\"

    Rule 3:

    Use measurable statements.

    Rule 4:

    Define exceptions.

    Example:

    Normal:

    Upload successful.

    Exception:

    Invalid file format.

    10. Common SRS Mistakes

    Mistake 1:

    Copying BRD into SRS.

    Mistake 2:

    Missing non-functional requirements.

    Mistake 3:

    Not defining integrations.

    Mistake 4:

    Ambiguous language.

    11. BRD → FRD → SRS Complete Example

    Business Goal:

    Improve pharmacovigilance efficiency.

    ↓

    BRD:

    Need AI support for safety review.

    ↓

    FRD:

    System shall generate AI case summaries.

    ↓

    SRS:

    System shall:

    • Accept PDF/DOCX files

    • Extract medical entities

    • Generate summary within 30 seconds

    • Display confidence score

    • Store audit history

    12. Interview Questions

    Q1. What is SRS?

    Answer:

    \"SRS is a detailed specification document that defines functional and non-functional requirements, system behavior, interfaces, constraints, and quality expectations.\"

    Q2. Difference between FRD and SRS?

    Answer:

    \"FRD defines required system functions, while SRS provides detailed technical and behavioral specifications of those functions.\"

    Q3. Is SRS required in Agile?

    Answer:

    \"Agile teams usually use lightweight documentation like user stories and acceptance criteria, but detailed SRS is still used in regulated industries.\"

    Practical Assignment

    Create SRS for:

    Feature:

    AI Case Risk Assessment Module

    Include:

    Functional Requirements

    Minimum 5:

    • Input

    • Processing

    • Output

    Non-functional Requirements

    Minimum 5:

    • Performance

    • Security

    • Availability

    • Scalability

    • Compliance

    Business Rules

    Minimum 3.
